Proofs and Refutations for Intuitionistic and Second-Order Logic (Extended Version)
Pablo Barenbaum, Teodoro Freund

Abstract
The λ PRK \lambda^{\textup{PRK}} -calculus is a typed λ \lambda -calculus that exploits the duality between the notions of proof and refutation to provide a computational interpretation for classical propositional logic. In this work, we extend λ PRK \lambda^{\textup{PRK}} to encompass classical second-order logic, by incorporating parametric polymorphism and existential types. The system is shown to enjoy good computational properties, such as type preservation, confluence, and strong normalization, which is established by means of a reducibility argument. We identify a syntactic restriction on proofs that characterizes exactly the intuitionistic fragment of second-order λ PRK \lambda^{\textup{PRK}} , and we study canonicity results.



Universidad de Buenos Aires, Argentina and Univeridad Nacional de Quilmes (CONICET), Argentinapbarenbaum@dc.uba.ar.orgUniversidad de Buenos Aires, Argentinatfreund95@gmail.com

This work was partially supported by project grant PUNQ 2247/22.

Theory of computation Type theory

1
Introduction

Constructivism in logic is closely related with the notion of
algorithm

in computer science.
The reason is that a constructive proof of existence of a mathematical object
fulfilling certain properties should provide an
effective construction

of such an object.
For example, a constructive proof of

\forall x\in\mathbb{N}.\,\exists y\in\mathbb{N}.\,P(x,y)

may be understood as an algorithm that takes as input a natural number
x

and produces as output a natural number
y
that verifies
P(x,y)
.
The close relationship that exists
between
proofs
and
computer programs
,
and between
logical propositions
and
program specifications

(or
types
),
can be taken to its maximum consequences
in the form of the
propositions-as-types correspondence
.

This correspondence has given rise to a broad and active area of research,
guided by the principle that

each proof-theoretical notion has a
computational counterpart and vice-versa
.
The interest is that this correspondence
allows logic and computer science to feed back on each other.
Indeed, besides minimal propositional logic,
it has been extended to such settings as

first-order logic

[
14
,
29
,
7
]
,

second-order logic

[
20
,
41
]
,

linear logic

[
21
]
,

modal logic

[
5
,
13
]

and
classical logic

[
22
,
9
,
3
,
36
]
.

The question of what kind of computational system would
constitute a reasonable counterpart for
classical logic
,
from the point of view of the propositions-as-types correspondence,
is far from being definitely settled.
This work is part of the quest for a satisfactory answer to this
problem.

The proofs and refutations calculus (
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
)

Until the late 1980s, it was widely thought that it was not possible
to extend the propositions-as-types correspondence to encompass classical
logic. This view changed when Griffin
[
22
]

remarked that the classical principle of double negation elimination
(
\neg\neg A\to A
) can be understood as the typing rule for
a control operator
\mathcal{C}
, closely related to Felleisen’s
\mathcal{C}

operator
[
18
]
and to
Scheme
’s
call/cc
.
Since then, many other calculi for classical logic have been proposed.
Significant examples are Parigot’s
\lambda\mu

[
36
]
,
Barbanera and Berardi’s
symmetric
\lambda
-calculus
[
3
]
,
and
Curien and Herbelin’s
\bar{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}
calculus
[
9
]
.

The starting point of this paper is the logical system
PRK
,
introduced recently by the authors
[
4
]

and extending Nelson’s constructive negation
[
33
]
.
In
PRK
, propositions become classified along two dimensions:
their
sign
, which may be positive or negative,
and their
strength
, which may be strong or weak.
This results into four possible
modes
to state

\begin{array}[]{l|ll}&\text{Positive}&\text{Negative}\\
\hline\cr\text{Strong}&A{}^{+}&A{}^{-}\\
\text{Weak}&A{}^{\oplus}&A{}^{\ominus}\end{array}

a proposition.
Positive (
A{}^{+}/A{}^{\oplus}
) and negative (
A{}^{-}/A{}^{\ominus}
)
propositions correspond to affirmations and
denials.
Strong (
A{}^{+}/A{}^{-}
)
and weak (
A{}^{\oplus}/A{}^{\ominus}
) propositions
impose restrictions on the shape of canonical proofs:
a canonical proof of a strong affirmation (
A{}^{+}
)
must always be constructed with an introduction rule
for the corresponding logical connective,
whereas a canonical proof of a weak affirmation (
A{}^{\oplus}
)
must always proceed by
reductio ad absurdum
,
by assuming the weak denial
A{}^{\ominus}

and proving the strong affirmation
A{}^{+}
.

We summarize some important characteristics of
PRK
.
First,

PRK
is a
refinement
of classical logic:

A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash B
holds in classical propositional logic
if and only if

A{}^{\oplus}_{1},\ldots,A{}^{\oplus}_{n}\vdash B{}^{\oplus}
holds in
PRK
.
In fact
PRK
is “finer” than classical logic:
for example,
the law of excluded middle holds
weakly
,

i.e.

(A\lor\neg A){}^{\oplus}
is valid in
PRK
,
whereas it does not hold
strongly
,

i.e.

(A\lor\neg A){}^{+}
is not valid (in general) in
PRK
.
Second,
the
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus, which results
from assigning proof terms to
PRK
proofs
and endowing it with rewrite rules,
turns out to be
confluent
and
strongly normalizing
,
besides enjoying
subject reduction
.
Third,
as a result,
PRK
enjoys
canonicity
:
a proof of a sequent
\vdash P

without assumptions can always be normalized to a

canonical
proof, headed by an introduction rule.

Contributions and structure of this paper

The
PRK
logical system of
[
4
]
only treats three propositional
connectives: conjunction, disjunction, and negation.

•

In Section
2
, we
extend the
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
calculus

to propositional second-order logic.
We incorporate

second-order universal and existential quantification
,
as well as two propositional connectives,
implication and co-implication.
The system is shown to
refine classical second-order logic
,
and to enjoy good computational properties:
subject reduction
and

confluence
.
This extension increases the expressivity of the system, allowing
to encode inductive datatypes such as natural numbers, lists, and trees.

•

In Section
3
,
we study
Böhm–Berarducci encodings
, that is,
we study how the logical connectives of second-order
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

may be encoded in terms of universal quantification and implication only
(
\{\forall,\rightarrow\}
).
The encoding turns out to be only partially satisfactory:
it simulates proof normalization for an introduction rule
followed by an elimination rule in the
positive
case but,
unfortunately, not in the negative case.

•

In Section
4
we prove
strong normalization

for the second-order
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus.
This is the most technically challenging part of the work.
In
[
4
]
, normalization of the propositional
fragment of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
is attained by means of a translation
to System F with non-strictly positive recursion.
This technique does not carry over
to the second-order case.
To prove strong normalization, we use a variant of Girard’s technique
of reducibility candidates and, in particular,
we resort to a non-trivial adaptation
of Mendler’s proof of strong normalization
for System F with non-strictly positive recursion
[
30
]
.

•

In Section
5
,
we define a subsystem of second-order
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
,
called
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}
, by imposing a syntactic restriction on terms.
We show that
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}

refines second-order intuitionistic logic
, in the sense
that
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}
is a conservative extension of second-order intuitionistic
logic and, conversely,
second-order intuitionistic logic can be embedded in
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}
.

•

In Section
6
we formulate
canonicity
results for
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
.
In particular, we strengthen the canonicity results of
[
4
]

to show that an explicit witness can be extracted from a proof of
P
.

•

Finally, in Section
7
we conclude and we discuss
some related and future work.

2
Second-Order Proofs and Refutations

In this section we define a second-order extension of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
,
including its syntax, typing rules, and rewriting rules.
We show that
the system enjoys
subject reduction
, it is
confluent
,
and it
refines classical second-order logic
(Thm.
3
).

Syntax of types

We assume given a denumerable set of

type variables

\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots
.
The sets of
pure types
(
A,B,\ldots
)
and
types
(
P,Q,\ldots
) are given by:

A::=\alpha\mid A\land A\mid A\lor A\mid A\rightarrow A\mid A\ltimes A\mid\neg A\mid\forall\alpha.\,A\mid\exists\alpha.\,A\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636ptP::=A{}^{+}\mid A{}^{-}\mid A{}^{\oplus}\mid A{}^{\ominus}

where
A\ltimes B
represents
co-implication
,
the dual connective to implication,
to be understood (roughly) as
\neg A\land B
.
The four modes represent strong affirmation (
A{}^{+}
),
strong denial (
A{}^{-}
), weak affirmation (
A{}^{\oplus}
),
and weak denial (
A{}^{\ominus}
).
Note that
modes
(
{}^{+},{}^{-},{}^{\oplus},{}^{\ominus}
) can only decorate the
root of a type,
i.e.
they cannot be nested.

Sometimes one may be interested in
fragments
of the system.
For instance, the
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus of
[
4
]

corresponds to the
\{\land,\lor,\neg\}
fragment.
In this paper we are usually interested in the full

\{\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\ltimes,\neg,\forall,\exists\}
fragment.
As long as there is little danger of confusion
we still speak of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
without further qualifications.

Syntax of terms

Terms of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
are given by the following grammar.
The letter
i
ranges over
\{1,2\}
.
Some terms are decorated with either “
+
” or “
-
”.
In the grammar we write “
±
”
to stand for either “
+
” or “
-
”.

\begin{array}[]{rllllll}t,s,\ldots::=&x^{P}&\text{variable}&\mid&t\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}s&\text{absurdity}\\
\mid&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{(x:P)}.\,t&\text{${}^{\oplus}/{}^{\ominus}$ introduction}&\mid&t\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,s&\text{${}^{\oplus}/{}^{\ominus}$ elimination}\\
\mid&\langle t,s\rangle^{\pm}&\text{$\land{}^{+}$/$\lor{}^{-}$ introduction}&\mid&\pi_{i}^{\pm}(t)&\text{$\land{}^{+}$/$\lor{}^{-}$ elimination}\\
\mid&\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\pm}(t)&\text{$\lor{}^{+}$/$\land{}^{-}$ introduction}&\mid&\delta^{\pm}t\,[_{x:P}.s][_{y:Q}.u]&\text{$\lor{}^{+}$/$\land{}^{-}$ elimination}\\
\mid&\lambda^{\pm}_{(x:P)}.{t}&\text{$\rightarrow{}^{+}$/$\ltimes{}^{-}$ introduction}&\mid&t@^{\pm}s&\text{$\rightarrow{}^{+}$/$\ltimes{}^{-}$ elimination}\\
\mid&(t\,\bm{;}\!^{\pm}s)&\text{$\ltimes{}^{+}$/$\rightarrow{}^{-}$ introduction}&\mid&\varrho^{\pm}{t}[_{x:P;y:Q}.{s}]&\text{$\ltimes{}^{+}$/$\rightarrow{}^{-}$ elimination}\\
\mid&\mathsf{N}^{\pm}t&\text{$\neg{}^{+}$/$\neg{}^{-}$ introduction}&\mid&\mathsf{M}^{\pm}t&\text{$\neg{}^{+}$/$\neg{}^{-}$ elimination}\\
\mid&\lambda^{\pm}_{\alpha}.\,t&\text{$\forall{}^{+}$/$\exists{}^{-}$ introduction}&\mid&t@^{\pm}A&\text{$\forall{}^{+}$/$\exists{}^{-}$ elimination}\\
\mid&\langle A,t\rangle^{\pm}&\text{$\exists{}^{+}$/$\forall{}^{-}$ introduction}&\mid&\nabla^{\pm}\,t[_{(\alpha,x:P)}.s]&\text{$\exists{}^{+}$/$\forall{}^{-}$ elimination}\end{array}

The notions of free and bound occurrences of variables are defined as
expected,
with the typographical convention that subscripted variable occurrences
are binding.
Terms are considered up to
\alpha
-renaming of bound variables.
We write
\mathsf{fv}(t)
for the set of free variables of
t

and
\mathsf{ftv}(t)
for the set of type variables occurring free in
t
.
By
t\{x:=s\}
we mean the capture-avoiding substitution
of the free occurrences of
x
in
t
by
s
.

Variables are formally annotated with their type, which we usually omit.
Sometimes we also omit the types of bound variables if they are clear
from the context, as well as the name of unused bound variables,
writing “
\underline{\,\,\,}
” instead. For example, if
x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(t)

we may write
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}}.\,t
rather than

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{-})}.\,t
.
Application-like operators are assumed to be left-associative;
for example,

t@{}^{+}s\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,u@{}^{+}A

stands for

((t@{}^{+}s)\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,u)@{}^{+}A
.
In a term of the form
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{(x:P)}.\,t
,
the variable
x
is called the
counterfactual
,
and more specifically a
negative counterfactual

in a term of the form
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,t
.
In a term of the form
t\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,s
,
we call
t
the
subject
and
s
the
argument
.
We write
\mathtt{C}
for arbitrary term
contexts
,

i.e.
terms with a single free occurrence
of a distinguished variable
\Box
called a
hole
.
We write
\mathtt{C}\langle t\rangle
for the variable-capturing substitution of the
hole of
\mathtt{C}
by
t
.

The
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
type system

A
typing context
, ranged over by
\Gamma,\Delta,\ldots
,
is a finite assignment of variables to types,
written as
x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n}
.
We write
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
for the
domain
of
\Gamma
,

i.e.
the finite set
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
.
Typing judgments in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
are of the form
\Gamma\vdash t:P
,
meaning that
t
has type
P
under the context
\Gamma
.
Derivable judgments are given inductively by the typing rules below.

We write
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P

if the typing judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:P

is derivable in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
.
When we wish to emphasize the logical point of view,
we may write sequents as
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\vdash Q
,
and we may write
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}Q
to mean
that there exists a term
t
such that

x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:Q
.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 27.28769pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=60.96698pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{Ax}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,x:P\vdash x:P$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{+}\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash s:A{}^{-}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=93.82504pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{Abs}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 19.1907pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}s:P$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash t:A{}^{+}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=68.94394pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\circ}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0678pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\ominus}}.\,t:A{}^{\oplus}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.56554pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash t:A{}^{-}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=68.80836pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\circ}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.\,t:A{}^{\ominus}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\oplus}\hskip 8.5359pt\Gamma\vdash s:A{}^{\ominus}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=91.75127pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\circ}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 17.46092pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s:A{}^{+}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\ominus}\hskip 8.5359pt\Gamma\vdash s:A{}^{\oplus}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=91.75127pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\circ}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 20.72757pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,s:A{}^{-}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\oplus}\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash s:B{}^{\oplus}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=97.95317pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\land}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 7.31732pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\langle t,s\rangle{}^{+}:(A\land B){}^{+}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\ominus}\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash s:B{}^{\ominus}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=97.95317pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\lor}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 10.58397pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\langle t,s\rangle{}^{-}:(A\lor B){}^{-}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=72.0008pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}^{+}_{\land{i}}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 4.34508pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\pi_{i}^{+}(t):A_{i}{}^{\oplus}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{-}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=68.73415pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}^{-}_{\lor{i}}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 4.2539pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\pi_{i}^{-}(t):A_{i}{}^{\ominus}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}\hskip 14.22636pti\in\{1,2\}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=93.91925pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}^{+}_{\lor{i}}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 1.80429pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{+}(t):(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{+}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A_{i}{}^{\ominus}\hskip 14.22636pti\in\{1,2\}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=93.91925pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}^{-}_{\land{i}}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 5.07094pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{-}(t):(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{-}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(A\lor B){}^{+}\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash s:P\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,y:B{}^{\oplus}\vdash u:P$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=223.03256pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\lor}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 57.04507pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\delta{}^{+}t\,[_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.s][_{y:B{}^{\oplus}}.u]:P$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(A\land B){}^{-}\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash s:P\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,y:B{}^{\ominus}\vdash u:P$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=219.76591pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\land}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 57.04507pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\delta{}^{-}t\,[_{x:A{}^{\ominus}}.s][_{y:B{}^{\ominus}}.u]:P$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 9.02725pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash t:B{}^{\oplus}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=87.6849pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\rightarrow}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.{t}:(A\rightarrow B){}^{+}$}}}}\end{array}\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 8.16475pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash t:B{}^{\ominus}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=85.9599pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\ltimes}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda{}^{-}_{x:A{}^{\ominus}}.{t}:(A\ltimes B){}^{-}$}}}}\end{array}\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(A\rightarrow B){}^{+}\hskip 8.5359pt\!\Gamma\vdash s:A{}^{\oplus}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=114.48196pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\rightarrow}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 27.4761pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t@{}^{+}s:B{}^{\oplus}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(A\ltimes B){}^{-}\hskip 8.5359pt\!\Gamma\vdash s:A{}^{\ominus}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=112.75697pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\ltimes}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 28.24693pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t@{}^{-}s:B{}^{\ominus}$}}}}\end{array}\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\ominus}\hskip 14.22636pt\!\Gamma\vdash s:B{}^{\oplus}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=96.45317pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\ltimes}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 4.3521pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{+}s):(A\ltimes B){}^{+}$}}}}\end{array}\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\oplus}\hskip 8.5359pt\!\Gamma\vdash s:B{}^{\ominus}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=90.76271pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\rightarrow}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 5.54434pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}s):(A\rightarrow B){}^{-}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(A\ltimes B){}^{+}\hskip 8.5359pt\!\!\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus},y:B{}^{\oplus}\vdash s:P$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=166.91908pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\ltimes}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 36.60617pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varrho^{+}{t}[_{x:A{}^{\ominus};y:B{}^{\oplus}}.{s}]:P$}}}}\end{array}\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(A\rightarrow B){}^{-}\hskip 8.5359pt\!\!\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus},y:B{}^{\ominus}\vdash s:P$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=162.11078pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\rightarrow}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 35.83534pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\varrho^{-}{t}[_{x:A{}^{\oplus};y:B{}^{\ominus}}.{s}]:P$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 12.54573pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\ominus}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=66.21477pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\lnot}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{N}{}^{+}t:(\neg A){}^{+}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 9.27908pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\oplus}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=59.68147pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\lnot}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{N}{}^{-}t:(\neg A){}^{-}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(\neg A){}^{+}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=55.85097pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\lnot}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 1.4111pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{M}{}^{+}t:A{}^{\ominus}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(\neg A){}^{-}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=52.58432pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\lnot}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 1.4111pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{M}{}^{-}t:A{}^{\oplus}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\oplus}\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=94.00255pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\forall}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 6.6625pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,t:(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{+}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{\ominus}\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=94.00255pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\exists}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 8.70741pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda{}^{-}_{\alpha}.\,t:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{-}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.29575pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{+}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=99.92365pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\forall}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t@{}^{+}A:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.29575pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{-}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=96.657pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\exists}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t@{}^{-}A:B{}^{\ominus}\{\alpha:=A\}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.00412pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=85.40984pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\exists}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\langle A,t\rangle{}^{+}:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{+}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:B{}^{\ominus}\{\alpha:=A\}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=79.4016pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{I${}_{\forall}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.26253pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\langle A,t\rangle{}^{-}:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{-}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{+}\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash s:P\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma,P)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=207.0036pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\exists}^{+}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 56.37341pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\nabla{}^{+}\,t[_{(\alpha,x:A{}^{\oplus})}.s]:P$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{-}\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash s:P\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma,P)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=203.73695pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{E${}_{\forall}^{-}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 56.3734pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\nabla{}^{-}\,t[_{(\alpha,x:A{}^{\ominus})}.s]:P$}}}}\end{array}

The typing rules may be informally explained as follows.

Ax
is the standard axiom.
The
absurdity rule
(
Abs
) allows to derive any conclusion
from a strong proof and a strong refutation of
A
.
Introduction and elimination rules for
weak affirmation and denial (
I
{}_{\circ}^{\pm}
,
E
{}_{\circ}^{\pm}
)
follow the principle that a weak affirmation
A{}^{\oplus}

behaves like an implication “
A{}^{\ominus}\to A{}^{+}
”.
Indeed,
I
{}_{\circ}^{+}
and
E
{}_{\circ}^{+}
have the same structure as the introduction
and the elimination rule for an implication “
A{}^{\ominus}\to A{}^{+}
”,
where
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{x}.\,t
and
t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s

are akin to
\lambda
-abstraction and application.
The intuition behind this is that
A{}^{\oplus}
is the type of
weak proofs of a proposition
A
,
where a weak proof proceeds by
reductio ad absurdum
,
assuming a weak refutation (
A{}^{\ominus}
),
and providing a
strong
proof (
A{}^{+}
).
Dually, a weak denial
A{}^{\ominus}
behaves like “
A{}^{\oplus}\to A{}^{-}
”.

The remaining rules are introduction and elimination rules
for positive and negative strong connectives.
These rules come in dual pairs:
for each rule for a connective with
positive sign there is a symmetric rule for the dual connective
with negative sign
.
For example, the introduction rule for positive conjunction (
I
{}_{\land}^{+}
)
states that to strongly prove
A\land B

it suffices to weakly prove
A
and weakly prove
B
.
Dually, the introduction rule for negative disjunction (
I
{}_{\lor}^{-}
)
states that to strongly refute
A\lor B

it suffices to weakly refute
A
and weakly refute
B
.

The introduction and elimination rules for most logical connectives
(
\land
,
\lor
,
\rightarrow
,
\ltimes
,
\forall
,
\exists
)
are mechanically derived from the standard natural deduction rules
following this methodology, taking in account that the dual pairs
of connectives are

(\land,\lor)
,
(\rightarrow,\ltimes)
, and
(\forall,\exists)
.
In general,

introduction rules have weak premises and strong conclusions
,
whereas
elimination rules have strong premises and weak conclusions
.

The typing rules for conjunction (
\textup{{I${}_{\land}^{+}$}},\textup{{E${}^{+}_{\land{i}}$}},\textup{{I${}^{-}_{\land{i}}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\land}^{-}$}}
),
disjunction (
\textup{{I${}^{+}_{\lor{i}}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\lor}^{+}$}},\textup{{I${}_{\lor}^{-}$}},\textup{{E${}^{-}_{\lor{i}}$}}
),
positive implication (
\textup{{I${}_{\rightarrow}^{+}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\rightarrow}^{+}$}}
),
negative co-implication (
\textup{{I${}_{\ltimes}^{-}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\ltimes}^{-}$}}
),
universal (
\textup{{I${}_{\forall}^{+}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\forall}^{+}$}},\textup{{I${}_{\forall}^{-}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\forall}^{-}$}}
)
and existential quantification (
\textup{{I${}_{\exists}^{+}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\exists}^{+}$}},\textup{{I${}_{\exists}^{-}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\exists}^{-}$}}
)
are typical, so for instance

\langle t,s\rangle^{\pm}
forms a pair,

\pi_{i}^{\pm}(t)
is the
i
-th projection,

\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\pm}(t)
is the
i
-th injection into a disjoint union type,

\delta^{\pm}t\,[_{x}.s][_{y}.u]

is a pattern matching construct, and so on.
The rules differ from usual typed
\lambda
-calculi
only in the signs and strengths that decorate premises and conclusions.

The typing rules for positive co-implication (
\textup{{I${}_{\ltimes}^{+}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\ltimes}^{+}$}}
),
sometimes called
subtraction

[
8
]
,
and negative implication (
\textup{{I${}_{\rightarrow}^{-}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\rightarrow}^{-}$}}
)
follow the rough interpretation of
A\ltimes B

as
\neg A\land B
,
so a strong proof of a co-implication
A\ltimes B

is given by a pair
(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{+}s)

comprising a weak refutation of
A
and a weak proof of
B
.
Dually, a strong refutation of
A\rightarrow B

is given by a pair
(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}s)

comprising a weak proof of
A
and a weak refutation of
B
.
The eliminators
\varrho^{\pm}{t}[_{x;y}.{s}]
are presented
as generalized elimination rules, in multiplicative style.

The typing rules for negation (
\textup{{I${}_{\lnot}^{+}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\lnot}^{+}$}},\textup{{I${}_{\lnot}^{-}$}},\textup{{E${}_{\lnot}^{-}$}}
) express
that to strongly prove
\neg A
is the same as to weakly refute
A
,
and dually for strong refutations of
\neg A
.

Example 1
.

Let
\top\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\forall\alpha.\,(\alpha\rightarrow\alpha)

and
\bot\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\forall\alpha.\,\alpha
.
Recall from
[
4
]
that
the weak non-contradiction principle
\Gamma\vdash(A\land\neg A){}^{\ominus}

holds in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
.
Then
\vdash\top{}^{\oplus}
and
\bot{}^{\oplus}\vdash A{}^{\oplus}

hold, where
A
stands for any pure type:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{2cm}}c}{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\hskip-5.69046pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\hskip-5.69046pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\hskip-5.69046pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\hskip-5.69046pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\\
&\,\textup{{Ax}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\top{}^{\ominus},(\alpha\rightarrow\alpha){}^{\ominus},\alpha{}^{\oplus}\vdash\alpha{}^{\oplus}\end{array}}\\
&\,\textup{{I${}_{\rightarrow}^{+}$}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\top{}^{\ominus},(\alpha\rightarrow\alpha){}^{\ominus}\vdash(\alpha\rightarrow\alpha){}^{+}\end{array}}\\
&\,\textup{{I${}_{\circ}^{+}$}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\top{}^{\ominus}\vdash(\alpha\rightarrow\alpha){}^{\oplus}\end{array}}\\
&\,\textup{{I${}_{\forall}^{+}$}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\top{}^{\ominus}\vdash\top{}^{+}\end{array}}\\
&\,\textup{{I${}_{\circ}^{+}$}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\vdash\top{}^{\oplus}\end{array}}\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\hskip-5.69046pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\\
&\,\textup{{Ax}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\bot{}^{\oplus}\vdash\bot{}^{\oplus}\end{array}}\hskip 14.22636pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\hskip-5.69046pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\hskip-5.69046pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\hskip-5.69046pt\text{(By weak non-contradiction.)}\\
&\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\bot{}^{\oplus},\bot{}^{\oplus}\vdash(B\land\neg B){}^{\ominus}\end{array}}\\
&\,\textup{{I${}_{\forall}^{-}$}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\bot{}^{\oplus},\bot{}^{\oplus}\vdash\bot{}^{-}\end{array}}\\
&\,\textup{{I${}_{\circ}^{-}$}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\bot{}^{\oplus}\vdash\bot{}^{\ominus}\end{array}}\\
&\,\textup{{Abs}}^{\prime}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\bot{}^{\oplus}\vdash A{}^{\oplus}\end{array}}\end{array}

The
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus

The opposite type
P{}^{\sim}
of a given type
P

is defined by flipping the sign,

i.e.

(A{}^{\oplus}){}^{\sim}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,A{}^{\ominus}
;

(A{}^{\ominus}){}^{\sim}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,A{}^{\oplus}
;

(A{}^{+}){}^{\sim}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,A{}^{-}
;
and
(A{}^{-}){}^{\sim}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,A{}^{+}
.
If
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:P{}^{\sim}

then a term
t\mathrel{\bowtie_{Q}}s
may be constructed
such that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t\mathrel{\bowtie_{Q}}s:Q
,
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rl@{\hspace{.5cm}}rl}t\mathrel{\bowtie_{Q}}s\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,t\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{Q}}}s&\text{if $P=A{}^{+}$}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&t\mathrel{\bowtie_{Q}}s\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,s\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{Q}}}t&\text{if $P=A{}^{-}$}\\
t\mathrel{\bowtie_{Q}}s\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{Q}}}(s\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,t)&\text{if $P=A{}^{\oplus}$}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&t\mathrel{\bowtie_{Q}}s\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,(s\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,t)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{Q}}}(t\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,s)&\text{if $P=A{}^{\ominus}$}\\
\end{array}

We endow typable
PRK
terms with a notion of reduction,
defining the
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus

by a binary rewriting relation
\xrightarrow{}

on typable
PRK
terms,
given by the rewriting rules below,
and closed by compatibility under arbitrary contexts.
Rules are presented following the convention that,
if many occurrences of “
\pm
” appear in the same expression,
they are all supposed to stand for the same sign:

{\small\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}l@{\,}l@{\,\,\,}@{\,\,\,}r@{\,}l@{\,}l}(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{x}.\,t)\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,s&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\circ}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\circ}}}&t\{x:=s\}\\
\pi_{i}^{\pm}(\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle^{\pm})&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\land}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\lor}}}&t_{i}&\delta^{\pm}(\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\pm}(t))\,[_{x}.s_{1}][_{x}.s_{2}]&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\lor}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\land}}}&s_{i}\{x:=t\}\\
(\lambda^{\pm}_{x}.{t})@^{\pm}s&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\rightarrow}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\ltimes}}}&t\{x:=s\}&\varrho^{\pm}{(t\,\bm{;}\!^{\pm}s)}[_{x;y}.{u}]&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\ltimes}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\rightarrow}}}&u\{x:=t\}\{y:=s\}\\
\mathsf{M}^{\pm}(\mathsf{N}^{\pm}t)&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\neg}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\neg}}}&t\\
(\lambda^{\pm}_{\alpha}.\,t)@^{\pm}A&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\forall}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\exists}}}&t\{\alpha:=A\}&\nabla^{\pm}\,\langle A,t\rangle^{\pm}[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\exists}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\forall}}}&s\{\alpha:=A\}\{x:=t\}\\
\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle{}^{+}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{-}(s)&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\land}}}}&t_{i}\mathrel{\bowtie}s&\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{+}(t)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\langle s_{1},s_{2}\rangle{}^{-}&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\lor}}}}&t\mathrel{\bowtie}s_{i}\\
\lambda{}^{+}_{x}.{t}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(s\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}u)&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\rightarrow}}}}&t\{x:=s\}\mathrel{\bowtie}u&(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{+}s)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\lambda{}^{-}_{x}.{u}&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\ltimes}}}}&s\mathrel{\bowtie}u\{x:=t\}\\
(\mathsf{N}{}^{+}t)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(\mathsf{N}{}^{-}s)&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\neg}}}}&t\mathrel{\bowtie}s\\
(\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,t)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\langle A,s\rangle{}^{-}&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\forall}}}}&t\{\alpha:=A\}\mathrel{\bowtie}s&\langle A,t\rangle{}^{+}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(\lambda{}^{-}_{\alpha}.\,s)&\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\exists}}}}&t\mathrel{\bowtie}s\{\alpha:=A\}\end{array}}

The
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus has two kinds of rules:
“
\beta
” rules, akin to proof normalization rules in natural deduction,
and “
\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}
” rules, akin to cut elimination rules in
sequent calculus.
The
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\circ}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\circ}}
rules are exactly like the standard
\beta
-rule
of the
\lambda
-calculus, with the difference that the
abstraction
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\ominus}}.\,t

is not an introduction of an implication “
A{}^{\ominus}\to A{}^{+}
”
but rather the introduction of a weak affirmation
A{}^{\oplus}
.
The
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\rightarrow}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\ltimes}}
rules also describe a similar behavior,
where
\lambda{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.{t}
is of type
(A\to B){}^{\oplus}
.
The remaining
\beta
rules are straightforward,
encoding projection (
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\land}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\lor}}
), pattern matching (
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\lor}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\land}}
),
etcetera.

The
\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}
rules simplify
an absurdity
(t\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}s)
as much as possible,
but they are never able to get rid of the absurdity.
Indeed, note that the right-hand side of all the
\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}
rules
include the generalized absurdity operator (
\mathrel{\bowtie}
),
which is in turn defined in terms of the absurdity operator
(
\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}
).
Recall, for example, that if
t:A{}^{\oplus}
and
s:A{}^{\ominus}

then

t\mathrel{\bowtie}s=(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(s\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,t)
.
This means that an instance of the absurdity
is a proof which provides
no relevant information
.
For example, if
P=(B\land C){}^{+}

then
\pi_{1}^{+}(t\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}s)
is a well-formed term
of type
B{}^{\oplus}
;
but the argument of the projection is a term
t\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}s

which may never become a pair
\langle p,q\rangle{}^{+}
.
This means that the normal form will be a “stuck”
term of the form
\pi_{1}^{+}(t^{\prime}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}s^{\prime})
.

Example 2

(Reduction in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
)
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:A{}^{\oplus}
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:A{}^{\ominus}
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&(\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(\alpha\rightarrow\alpha){}^{\ominus}}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{x:\alpha{}^{\oplus}}.{x})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\langle A,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(\alpha\rightarrow\alpha){}^{\oplus}}.\,(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}s)\rangle{}^{-}\\
\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\forall}}}}&(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\rightarrow A){}^{\ominus}}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.{x})\mathrel{\bowtie}(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\rightarrow A){}^{\oplus}}.\,(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}s))\\
=&((\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\rightarrow A){}^{\ominus}}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.{x})\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\rightarrow A){}^{\oplus}}.\,(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}s)))\,{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}\\
&((\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\rightarrow A){}^{\oplus}}.\,(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}s))\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\rightarrow A){}^{\ominus}}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.{x}))\\
\mathrel{\xrightarrow{{\small\mathsf{\beta^{\pm}_{\circ}}}}\!\!{}^{*}\,\,}&(\lambda{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.{x})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}s)\,\,\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\rightarrow}}}}\,\,t\mathrel{\bowtie}s=(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(s\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,t)\end{array}

An
\eta
-like rewriting rule can also be incorporated to
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

as done in
[
4
, Thm. 37]
,
declaring that

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{x}.\,(t\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,x)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\eta_{\circ}}}t

if
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)
.
The
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus (with or without the
\mathsf{\eta_{\circ}}
rule)
enjoys the following properties:

Theorem 3
.

1.

Subject reduction.

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P
and
t\xrightarrow{}s
,
then
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:P
.

2.

Confluence.

The
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus has the Church–Rosser property.

3.

Classical refinement.

A{}^{\oplus}_{1},\ldots,A{}^{\oplus}_{n}\vdash B{}^{\oplus}

holds in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

if and only if

A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash B

holds in the classical second-order natural deduction system
NK
.

Proof.

Subject reduction

is a straightforward extension of
[
4
, Prop. 24]
,
with minor adaptations to account for
implication, co-implication,
and second-order quantification.

Confluence
follows from the fact that
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

can be modeled as an orthogonal higher-order rewriting system
in the sense of Nipkow
[
34
]
.

Classical refinement

is an extension of Prop. 38 and Thm. 39 from
[
4
]
;
this theorem has two parts:

•

The “only if” direction
(
A{}^{\oplus}_{1},\ldots,A{}^{\oplus}_{n}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}B{}^{\oplus}
implies

A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}B
)
means that
PRK
is a
conservative extension

of classical second-order logic.
To prove this statement, we generalize the statement as follows:
if
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}Q

then
\iota(P_{1}),\ldots,\iota(P_{n})\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\iota(Q)
,
where
\iota(A{}^{\oplus})=\iota(A{}^{+})=A

and
\iota(A{}^{\ominus})=\iota(A{}^{-})=\neg A
.
This can be shown by a straightforward induction on the derivation
of the first judgment.

•

The “if” direction
(
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}B
implies

A{}^{\oplus}_{1},\ldots,A{}^{\oplus}_{n}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}B{}^{\oplus}
)
means that classical logic can be
embedded
into
PRK
.
The essence of the proof is showing that all the inference rules of
classical second-order natural deduction are admissible
in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
, taking the weak affirmation of all propositions
(
i.e.
decorating all formulas with “
⊕
”).
Some cases are subtle, especially elimination rules.
Here we show the introduction and elimination rules for quantifiers
(
see Sections
B
,

C
in the appendix for complete
proofs
):

1.

Universal introduction.

Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus}
,
and define
t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,A
as the following term:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(B\{\alpha:=A\}){}^{\ominus})}.\,((t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus})}.\,\langle A,x\rangle{}^{-})@{}^{+}A\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x)
.
Then we have that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,A:B\{\alpha:=A\}{}^{\oplus}
.

2.

Universal elimination.

Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus}
.
Define
t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,A
as the following term:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(B\{\alpha:=A\}){}^{\ominus})}.\,((t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus})}.\,\langle A,x\rangle{}^{-})@{}^{+}A\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x)
.
Then we have that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,A:B\{\alpha:=A\}{}^{\oplus}
.

3.

Existential introduction.

Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(B\{\alpha:=A\}){}^{\oplus}
.
Define

\langle A,t\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}

as the following term:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{\ominus})}.\,\langle A,t\rangle{}^{+}
.
Then we have that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\langle A,t\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus}
.

4.

Existential elimination.

Let

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{\oplus}

and

\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:B{}^{\oplus}

with
\alpha\not\in\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma,P)
.
Define

\nabla^{\mathcal{C}}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]

as the following term:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:B{}^{\ominus})}.\,(\nabla{}^{+}\,t^{\prime}[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y)

where
t^{\prime}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{\oplus})}.\,\lambda{}^{-}_{\alpha}.\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.\,(s\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{-}}}y)
.
Then

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\nabla^{\mathcal{C}}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]:B{}^{\oplus}
.
∎

3
Böhm–Berarducci Encodings

It is well-known that, in System F, logical connectives
such as
\top
,
\bot
,
\land
,
\lor
,
\exists
,
as well as inductive data types,
can be represented using only
\forall
and
\rightarrow

by means of their
Böhm–Berarducci encodings

[
6
]
,
which can be understood as

universal properties
or
structural induction principles
.
Böhm–Berarducci encodings can be reproduced in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
.
In the following subsections we study the encoding of connectives
in terms of universal quantification and implication.

The encoding of conjunction, for instance, can be taken to be

A\land B\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\forall\alpha.\,((A\to B\to\alpha)\to\alpha)
.
Then
positive typing rules for conjunction
,
analogous to
I
{}_{\land}^{+}
and
E
{}^{+}_{\land{i}}

are derivable, and their constructions simulate the
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\land}}
rule.
Indeed, let
X:=(A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\rightarrow A_{i})\rightarrow\alpha

and
Y:=A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\rightarrow\alpha
.
Moreover,
let
X_{i}:=(A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\rightarrow A_{i})\rightarrow A_{i}

and
Y_{i}:=A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\rightarrow A_{i}
.
Given
\Gamma\vdash t_{1}:A_{1}{}^{\oplus}
and
\Gamma\vdash t_{2}:A_{2}{}^{\oplus}

and
\Gamma\vdash s:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}
, define:

•

\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle{}^{+}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:X{}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(x:Y{}^{\oplus})}.{\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:\alpha{}^{\ominus})}.\,x\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:Y{}^{\oplus})}.\,(t_{1}\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}u))@{}^{+}t_{1}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,u@{}^{+}t_{2}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y}
,

where

u\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(B\to\alpha){}^{\oplus})}.\,(t_{2}\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}y)
.

•

\pi_{i}^{+}(s)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A_{i}{}^{\ominus})}.\,(t_{1}@{}^{+}A_{i}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:X_{i}{}^{\oplus})}.\,(r\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}x))@{}^{+}r\bullet x)
,

where

r\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:Y_{i}{}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(y_{1}:A_{1}{}^{\oplus})}.{\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A_{2}\rightarrow A_{i}){}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(y_{2}:A_{2}{}^{\oplus})}.{y_{i}}}
.

Then
\Gamma\vdash\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle{}^{+}:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}

and
\Gamma\vdash\pi_{i}^{+}(s):A_{i}{}^{\oplus}

and it can be easily checked that

\pi_{i}^{+}(\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle{}^{+})\mathrel{\rightarrow^{*}}t_{i}

(using
\mathsf{\eta_{\circ}}
).

On the other hand,
negative typing rules for conjunction
,
analogous to
I
{}^{-}_{\land{i}}
and a weak variant of
E
{}_{\land}^{-}
can also be derived.
First note that, given terms
\Gamma\vdash p:(A\to B){}^{\oplus}

and
\Gamma\vdash q:A{}^{\oplus}
,
a term
p\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,q
may be defined
in such a way that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}p\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,q:B{}^{\oplus}
.
An explicit construction is

p\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,q\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:B{}^{\ominus})}.\,(p\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\to B){}^{\oplus})}.\,(q\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}x))@{}^{+}q\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x)
.
Then we may encode
I
{}^{-}_{\land{i}}
and a weak variant of
E
{}_{\land}^{-}

as follows:

•

\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{-}(t)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\langle A_{i},\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:X_{i}{}^{\oplus})}.\,(r\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}t)\rangle{}^{-}
,

where

r\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:Y_{i}{}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(y_{1}:A_{1}{}^{\oplus})}.{\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A_{2}\rightarrow A_{i}){}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(y_{2}:A_{2}{}^{\oplus})}.{y_{i}}}
.

•

\delta{}^{-}t\,[_{a_{1}}.s_{1}][_{a_{2}}.s_{2}]\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\nabla{}^{-}\,t[_{(\alpha,x:X{}^{\ominus})}.\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(c:C{}^{\oplus})}.\,s^{\prime}_{1}\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,c]
,

where

s^{\prime}_{1}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,s_{1}\{a_{1}:=\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(z_{1}:A_{1}{}^{\oplus})}.\,(s^{\prime}_{2}\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{-}}}c)\}
,
and

s^{\prime}_{2}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,s_{2}\{a_{2}:=\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(z_{2}:A_{2}{}^{\oplus})}.\,(u\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{-}}}x)\}
,
and

u\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:X{}^{\ominus}}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{y:Y{}^{\oplus}}.{(y\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,z_{1}\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,z_{2})}
.

Note that
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{-}(t):(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{-}

and
\Gamma\vdash\delta{}^{-}t\,[_{a_{1}}.s_{1}][_{a_{2}}.s_{2}]:C{}^{\ominus}
.
However, unfortunately, it is

not
the case that

\delta{}^{-}\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{-}(t)\,[_{a_{1}}.s_{1}][_{a_{2}}.s_{2}]\mathrel{\rightarrow^{*}}s_{i}\{a_{i}:=t\}
;
in fact the computation becomes stuck.

In general, these kinds of encodings are able to simulate reduction
for the positive half of the system but not for the negative half
1
1

1

Naturally,
one may consider dual encodings in terms of
\exists
and
\ltimes
,
for example

A\lor B=\exists\alpha.\,((A\ltimes B\ltimes\alpha)\ltimes\alpha)
,
which behave well only for the negative half of the system.
.
This seems to suggest that
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
cannot be fully
simulated by the
\{\forall,\rightarrow\}
fragment, although we do not
know of a proof of this fact and there might exist
other encodings which allow simulating the full
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
calculus.

In Section
D
of the appendix,
encodings for (positive) disjunction and existential quantification
in terms of
\{\forall,\rightarrow\}
are also studied.

4
Normalization of Second-Order
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

In this section we construct a
reducibility model

for
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
and we prove
adequacy
(Thm.
5
) of the model,
from which
strong normalization

of second-order
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
follows.
We only discuss the proof of strong normalization for the calculus without
the
\mathsf{\eta_{\circ}}
rule
2
2

2

Strong
normalization for the full
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus with the
\mathsf{\eta_{\circ}}
rule comes
out as a relatively easy corollary by postponing
\mathsf{\eta_{\circ}}
steps
(see
e.g.

[
4
, Theorem 37]
for a similar result).
.

In
[
4
]
, strong normalization for the propositional fragment
of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
is shown via a translation to System F extended
with recursive type constraints
enjoying a (non-strict) positivity condition.
This technique does not seem to extend to the second-order case.
The problem is that the translation given in
[
4
]

is
not closed under type substitution
.
More precisely, if we denote the translation by
\{\!\!\{-\}\!\!\}
,
an equality such that

\{\!\!\{t\{\alpha:=A\}\}\!\!\}=\{\!\!\{t\}\!\!\}\{\alpha:=\{\!\!\{A\}\!\!\}\}

does not hold in general, making the proof fail.

Our proof of strong normalization is based on an adaptation of
Girard’s technique of reducibility candidates.
Specifically, we adapt Mendler’s proof of strong normalization
for the extended System F given in
[
30
]
.
We begin by defining an
untyped
version of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
:

The untyped
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus (
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}_{\mathbf{U}}
)

By
\mathbf{U}
we denote the set of
untyped terms
,
given by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rrl}a,b,c,\ldots&::=&x\mid a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b\mid\langle a,b\rangle\mid\pi_{i}(a)\mid\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)\mid\delta a\,[_{x}.b][_{y}.c]\mid\lambda_{x}.\,a\mid a@b\mid(a\,\bm{;}b)\mid\varrho{a}[_{x;y}.{b}]\\
&\mid&\mathsf{N}a\mid\mathsf{M}a\mid\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a\mid a@\Diamond\mid\langle\Diamond,a\rangle\mid\nabla\,a[_{(\Diamond,x)}.b]\end{array}

The reduction relation
\to_{\mathbf{U}}\subseteq\mathbf{U}\times\mathbf{U}

of the
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}_{\mathbf{U}}
-calculus
is defined by the following reduction rules,
closed by compatibility under arbitrary reduction contexts:

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\HS}rcl}\pi_{i}(\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle)&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&a_{i}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\delta(\mathsf{in}_{i}(a))\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&b_{i}\{x:=a\}\\
(\lambda_{x}.\,a)@b&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&a\{x:=b\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\varrho{(a_{1}\,\bm{;}a_{2})}[_{x;y}.{b}]&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&b\{x:=a_{1}\}\{y:=a_{2}\}\\
\mathsf{M}(\mathsf{N}a)&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&a\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt\\
(\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a)@\Diamond&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&a\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\nabla\,\langle\Diamond,a\rangle[_{(\Diamond,x)}.b]&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&b\{x:=a\}\\
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\mathsf{in}_{i}(b)&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&a_{i}\mathrel{\bowtie}b\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&a\mathrel{\bowtie}b_{i}\\
\lambda_{x}.\,a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(b\,\bm{;}c)&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&a\{x:=b\}\mathrel{\bowtie}c\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&(a\,\bm{;}b)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\lambda_{x}.\,c&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&b\mathrel{\bowtie}c\{x:=a\}\\
(\mathsf{N}a)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(\mathsf{N}b)&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&b\mathrel{\bowtie}a\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\langle\Diamond,b\rangle&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&a\mathrel{\bowtie}b\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\langle\Diamond,a\rangle\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,b&\to_{\mathbf{U}}&a\mathrel{\bowtie}b\end{array}

where

a\mathrel{\bowtie}b\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,(a@b)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(b@a)
.
The set
\mathbf{CAN}\subseteq\mathbf{U}
of

canonical terms
is the set of terms
built with a constructor,
i.e.
of any of the forms:

\langle a,b\rangle
,

\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)
,

\lambda_{x}.\,a
,

(a\,\bm{;}b)
,

\mathsf{N}a
,

\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a
,

\langle\Diamond,a\rangle
.

Note that the untyped calculus
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}_{\mathbf{U}}
is obtained from
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
by
erasing all signs and type annotations from terms,
replacing types and type variables by a placeholder “
\Diamond
’’
in introductors and eliminators for quantifiers,
and identifying
3
3

3

This identification is not essential,
but just a matter of syntactic economy.

“weak” abstraction and application
(
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}_{x}.\,t
and
t\bullet s
)
with regular abstraction and application
(
\lambda_{x}.\,t
and
t@s
).
It is easy to note that the
\to_{\mathbf{U}}
reduction is confluent,
observing that it can be modeled as an
orthogonal higher-order rewriting system
[
34
]
.

One difficult aspect of the strong normalization proof is that
terms of type
A{}^{\oplus}
behave as functions “
A{}^{\ominus}\to A{}^{+}
”
and, dually,
terms of
A{}^{\ominus}
behave as functions “
A{}^{\oplus}\to A{}^{-}
”.
Consequently,
sets of
reducible terms
cannot be defined by straightforward recursion,
as this would lead to a non-well-founded mutual dependency
between reducible terms of types
A{}^{\oplus}
and
A{}^{\ominus}
.
To address this difficulty, we follow Mendler’s approach
of taking fixed points in the

complete lattice of reducibility candidates
.

4.1
A Reducibility Model for
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

We begin by recalling a few standard notions from order theory.
A
complete lattice
is a partially ordered set
(A,\leq)

such that every subset
B\subseteq A

has a least upper bound and a greatest lower bound,
denoted respectively by
\bigvee{B}
and
\bigwedge{B}
.
Then (see
[
11
, Thm. 2.35]
):

Theorem 4

(Knaster–Tarski fixed point theorem)
.

If
(A,\leq)
is a complete lattice and

f:A\to A
is an order-preserving map,
i.e.
a\leq a^{\prime}\implies f(a)\leq f(a^{\prime})
,
then
f
has a least fixed point
and a greatest fixed point, given respectively by:

\bm{\mu}(f)=\bigwedge\{a\in A\ |\ f(a)\leq a\}

and

\bm{\nu}(f)=\bigvee\{a\in A\ |\ a\leq f(a)\}
.

We write

\bm{\mu}(\xi.f(\xi))
for
\bm{\mu}(f)

and

\bm{\nu}(\xi.f(\xi))
for
\bm{\nu}(f)
.

Reducibility candidates

Let
\mathbf{SN}\subseteq\mathbf{U}
denote the set of

strongly normalizing
terms, with respect to
\to_{\mathbf{U}}
.
A set
\xi\subseteq\mathbf{SN}
is
closed by reduction

if for every
a,b\in\mathbf{U}
such that
a\in\xi

and
a\to_{\mathbf{U}}b
,
one has that
b\in\xi
.
A set
\xi\subseteq\mathbf{SN}
is
complete

if for every
a\in\mathbf{SN}
the following holds:

(
\forall b\in\mathbf{CAN}.\ ((a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b)\implies b\in\xi
))
implies
a\in\xi

A set
\xi\subseteq\mathbf{SN}
is a
reducibility candidate

(or a r.c. for short)
if it is closed by reduction and complete.
We write
\mathbf{RC}
for the set of all r.c.’s,
that is,

\mathbf{RC}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\{\xi\subseteq\mathbf{SN}\ |\ \xi\text{ is a r.c. }\}
.

It is easy to see that reducibility candidates are non-empty.
In particular, for every
\xi\in\mathbf{RC}

we have that any variable
x\in\xi

is strongly normalizing and it vacuously verifies the property

\forall c\in\mathbf{CAN}.\ ((x\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c)\implies c\in\xi)

so, since
\xi
is complete, we have that
x\in\xi
.
Moreover, the set
\mathbf{RC}
forms a complete lattice
ordered by inclusion
\subseteq
.
Following Mendler
[
30
, Prop. 2]
,
the greatest lower bound of
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in{I}}

is given by the intersection
\cap_{i\in{I}}{\xi_{i}}
,
and the bottom element is the set

\bm{\bot}=\{a\in\mathbf{SN}\ |\ \nexists b\in\mathbf{CAN}.\,a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b\}

of terminating terms that have no canonical reduct.

See Section
E
in the appendix for details.

Operations on reducibility candidates

For each set of canonical terms
X\subseteq\mathbf{CAN}
, we define its

closure

\mathbb{C}{X}

as the set of all strongly normalizing terms
whose canonical reducts are in
X
. More precisely,

\mathbb{C}{X}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\{a\in\mathbf{SN}\ |\ \forall b\in\mathbf{CAN}.((a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b)\implies b\in X)\}
.
If
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
are r.c.’s and if
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in{I}}
is a set of r.c.’s, we define
the following operations:

{\small\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\HS}rcl}(\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2})&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathbb{C}{\{\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\ |\ a_{1}\in\xi_{1},a_{2}\in\xi_{2}\}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&(\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2})&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathbb{C}{\{\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)\ |\ i\in\{1,2\},a\in\xi_{i}\}}\\
(\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2})&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\{a\in\mathbf{SN}\ |\ \forall b\in\xi_{1}.\ a@b\in\xi_{2}\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&(\xi_{1}\bm{\ltimes}\xi_{2})&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathbb{C}{\{(a_{1}\,\bm{;}a_{2})\ |\ a_{1}\in\xi_{1},a_{2}\in\xi_{2}\}}\\
\bm{\mathord{\sim}}\xi&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathbb{C}{\{\mathsf{N}a\ |\ a\in\xi\}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt\\
\bm{\Pi}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\{a\in\mathbf{SN}\ |\ \forall i\in{I}.\ a@\Diamond\in\xi_{i}\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\bm{\Sigma}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathbb{C}{\{\langle\Diamond,a\rangle\ |\ \exists i\in I.\ a\in\xi_{i}\}}\end{array}}

It can be checked that all these operations
map r.c.’s to r.c.’s.

See Section
E
for details.

A straightforward observation is that the arrow operator
is order-reversing on the left,

i.e.
that
if
\xi_{1}\subseteq\xi^{\prime}_{1}

then
(\xi^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2})\subseteq(\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2})
.

Orthogonality

The idea of the normalization proof is, as usual, to associate, to each type
P
,
a set of
reducible terms

[\![P]\!]\in\mathbf{RC}
.
The interpretation of a type variable,
such as
[\![\alpha{}^{+}]\!]
or
[\![\alpha{}^{-}]\!]

shall be given by an
environment

\rho
,
mapping type variables to r.c.’s.
However, the sets
[\![\alpha{}^{+}]\!]
and
[\![\alpha{}^{-}]\!]

should not be chosen independently of each other
:
we require them to be orthogonal in the following sense.

Two reducibility candidates

\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
are
orthogonal
,
if for all
a_{1}\in\xi_{1}
and
a_{2}\in\xi_{2}

we have that
(a_{1}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}a_{2})\in\mathbf{SN}
.
We write
\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
for the set of all pairs
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbf{RC}^{2}

such that
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are orthogonal.

Reducible terms

The set of reducible terms is defined by induction on the following
notion of
measure

\#(-)
of a type
P
,
given by
\#(A{}^{+})=\#(A{}^{-})\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,2|A|

and
\#(A{}^{\oplus})=\#(A{}^{\ominus})\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,2|A|+1
,
where
|A|
denotes the
size
,
i.e.
the number of symbols,
of the pure type
A
.
Note for example that

\#((A\land B){}^{\oplus})>\#((A\land B){}^{+})>\#(A{}^{\oplus})
.

An
environment

is a function
\rho

mapping each type variable
\alpha^{\pm}
,
with either positive or negative sign,
to a reducibility candidate

\rho(\alpha^{\pm})\in\mathbf{RC}
,
in such a way that
(\rho(\alpha{}^{+}),\rho(\alpha{}^{-}))\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.
If
(\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
,
we write
\rho[\alpha:=\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-}]

for the environment
\rho^{\prime}
that extends
\rho

in such a way that
\rho^{\prime}(\alpha{}^{+})=\xi{}^{+}

and
\rho^{\prime}(\alpha{}^{-})=\xi{}^{-}

and
\rho^{\prime}(\beta^{\pm})=\rho(\beta^{\pm})

for any other type variable
\beta\neq\alpha
.

Given an environment
\rho
,
we define the set of
reducible terms
of type
P

under the environment
\rho
,
written
[\![P]\!]_{\rho}
,
by induction on the measure
\#(P)

as follows:

{\small\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l}[\![\alpha{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\rho(\alpha{}^{+})&[\![\alpha{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\rho(\alpha{}^{-})\\
[\![(A\land B){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\times}[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}&[\![(A\land B){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{+}[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\\
[\![(A\lor B){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{+}[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}&[\![(A\lor B){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\times}[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\\
[\![(A\rightarrow B){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}&[\![(A\rightarrow B){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\ltimes}[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\\
[\![(A\ltimes B){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\ltimes}[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}&[\![(A\ltimes B){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\\
[\![(\neg A){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\bm{\mathord{\sim}}[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}&[\![(\neg A){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\bm{\mathord{\sim}}[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\\
[\![(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\bm{\Pi}_{(\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}}\,{[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-}]}}&[\![(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\bm{\Sigma}_{(\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}}\,{[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-}]}}\\
[\![(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\bm{\Sigma}_{(\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}}\,{[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-}]}}&[\![(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\bm{\Pi}_{(\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}}\,{[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-}]}}\\
[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\bm{\mu}(\xi.((\xi\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}))&[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\bm{\nu}(\xi.((\xi\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}))\end{array}}

It is straightforward to check
for each type
P
and each environment
\rho

that
[\![P]\!]_{\rho}
is a reducibility candidate,
by induction on the measure
\#(P)
.
In the case of
[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}
,
by the Knaster–Tarski theorem (Thm.
4
),
to see that the least fixed point exists,
it suffices to observe that the mapping

f(\xi)=((\xi\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})

is order-preserving.
The case of
[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}
is similar.

Adequacy of the reducibility model

For each term
t
of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
,
we define an untyped term
|t|
of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}_{\mathbf{U}}

via the obvious forgetful map.
For instance
|\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(\exists\alpha.\,\alpha){}^{\ominus})}.\,\langle B,z^{B{}^{\oplus}}\rangle{}^{+}|=\lambda_{x}.\,\langle\Diamond,z\rangle
.
Note that each reduction step
t\xrightarrow{}s
in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

is mapped to a reduction step
|t|\to_{\mathbf{U}}|s|

in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}_{\mathbf{U}}
.
Hence, if
|t|
is strongly normalizing with respect to
\to_{\mathbf{U}}
,
then
t
is strongly normalizing with respect to
\xrightarrow{}
.

A
substitution
is a function
\sigma
mapping
each variable to a term in
\mathbf{U}
.
We write
a^{\sigma}
for the term that results from
the capture-avoiding substitution
of each free occurrence of each variable
x
in
a

by
\sigma(x)
.
We say that the substitution
\sigma
is

adequate
for the typing context
\Gamma
under the environment
\rho
,
and we write
\sigma\vDash_{\rho}\Gamma
,
if for each type assignment
(x:P)\in\Gamma

we have that
\sigma(x)\in[\![P]\!]_{\rho}
.
We are finally able to state the key result:

Theorem 5

(Adequacy)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash t:P

and
\sigma\vDash_{\rho}\Gamma

then
|t|^{\sigma}\in[\![P]\!]_{\rho}
.

The proof of the adequacy theorem relies on a number of auxiliary lemmas
stating properties such as

[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}=[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}

and

([\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho},[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.

See Section
E
in the appendix for the detailed proof.

From this we obtain as an easy corollary
that the
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus is strongly normalizing,
taking
\rho
as the environment that maps all type variables
to the bottom reducibility candidate,
and
\sigma
as the identity substitution.

5
Intuitionistic Proofs and Refutations

In natural deduction, it is well-known that classical logic can be
obtained from the intuitionistic system by adding a single classical
axiom, such as excluded middle or double negation elimination.
In sequent calculus, it is well-known that
intuitionistic logic can be obtained by restricting sequents

A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}

to have at most one formula on the right.
As we have seen,
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
refines classical logic.
It is a natural question to ask what subsystem of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

corresponds to intuitionistic logic.

In this section we characterize a restricted subsystem
of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
that corresponds to intuitionistic logic,
called
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}
,
by imposing a syntactic restriction on the shape of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

proofs, that forbids certain specific patterns of reasoning.
In particular, a variable
x
introduced by a
positive weak introduction
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{x}.\,t

can only occur free in
t
inside the arguments of weak eliminations.
The main result in this section is that

\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}
refines intuitionistic second-order
logic
(Thm.
9
).

As mentioned before, proofs of
strong
propositions in
PRK
must be
constructive.
However, this is only true for the toplevel logical connective in the
formula.
In general, a proof of
A{}^{+}
in
PRK
does not necessarily correspond
to an intuitionistic proof of
A
.
For example, a canonical proof of
(A\land B){}^{+}

is given by a proof of
A{}^{\oplus}
and a proof of
B{}^{\oplus}
,
but these subproofs may resort to classical reasoning principles.

The key to identify an intuitionistic subset of

\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
is to disallow inference rules which embody
classical principles.
One example is the
E
{}_{\lnot}^{-}
rule, which derives
A{}^{\oplus}
from
(\neg A){}^{-}
.
This rule embodies the classical principle
of double negation elimination (
\neg\neg A\to A
).
Another important example is the
I
{}_{\circ}^{+}
rule,
which derives
A{}^{\oplus}
from
A{}^{\ominus}\vdash A{}^{+}
.
This rule embodies the classical principle of

consequentia mirabilis
(
(\neg A\to A)\to A
).

The analysis of the
I
{}_{\circ}^{+}
rule suggests that,
in the intuitionistic fragment,
A{}^{\oplus}

should not be identified with “
A{}^{\ominus}\to A{}^{+}
”,
but directly with
A{}^{+}
.
One natural idea would be to impose an invariant over terms
of the form
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,t
,
in such a way that the body
t
may have no free occurrences
of the negative counterfactual
x
.
With this invariant,

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A{}^{+}\hskip 14.22636ptx\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=93.27477pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{$\textup{{I${}_{\circ}^{+}$}}$ {(variant)}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 6.00891pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,t:A{}^{\oplus}$}}}}\end{array}

all instances of the
I
{}_{\circ}^{+}
rule are actually instances of the
variant of
I
{}_{\circ}^{+}
shown on the right.
This in turn means that, in an application
t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s
,
the argument
s
is
useless
.
Indeed, if
t
becomes
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,t^{\prime}
,
the invariant ensures that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t^{\prime})
, so

(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,t^{\prime})\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s\to t^{\prime}
,
which does not depend on the specific choice of
s
.

Rather than completely forbidding classical reasoning principles,
we relax this condition so that classical principles
are allowed as long as they are useless,
i.e.
inside the argument
of an application
t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s
.
Furthermore, the invariant over terms of the form

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,t
, requesting that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)
,
can also be relaxed, in such a way that
x
is allowed to occur in
t

as long as all of its free occurrences are useless.
Formally:

Definition 6

(Intuitionistic terms)
.

A subterm of a term
t
is said to be
useless

if it lies inside the argument of a positive weak elimination.
More precisely, given a term
t=\mathtt{C}\langle s\rangle
,
we say that the subterm
s
under the context
\mathtt{C}

is useless if and only if there exist contexts
\mathtt{C}_{1},\mathtt{C}_{2}

and a term
u
such that
\mathtt{C}
is of the form

\mathtt{C}_{1}\langle u\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathtt{C}_{2}\langle\Box\rangle\rangle
.
A subterm of
t
is
useful
if it is not useless.
A term is said to be
intuitionistic
if and only if the
two following conditions hold:

1.

Useless negative eliminations (
E
{}_{\land}^{-}
,
E
{}_{\rightarrow}^{-}
,
E
{}_{\lnot}^{-}
,
E
{}_{\forall}^{-}
).

There are no useful subterms of any of the following forms:

\delta{}^{-}t\,[_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.s][_{(y:B{}^{\ominus})}.u]
,

\varrho^{-}{t}[_{(x:A{}^{\oplus});(y:B{}^{\ominus})}.{s}]
,

\mathsf{M}{}^{-}t
,

\nabla{}^{-}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]
.

2.

Useless negative counterfactuals.

In every useful subterm of the form
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,t
,
there are no useful occurrences of
x
in
t
.

The
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}
type system

The type system
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}

is defined by imposing the restriction
on
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
that terms be intuitionistic.
More precisely,
we say that a judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:P

holds in
PRJ
,
and in this case we write
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t:P
,
if the judgment holds in
PRK
and furthermore
t

is an intuitionistic term.
We also write
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}Q

if there exists a term
t
such that

x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n}\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t:Q
.

Example 7
.

The weak variant of the law of excluded middle,
(A\lor\neg A){}^{\oplus}
,
can be proven in
PRK
. For example, if we define
\pitchfork^{+}_{A}
:

\!\!{\small\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l}\pitchfork^{+}_{A}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(A\lor\neg A){}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{2}\!\!{}^{+}(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:\neg A{}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{+}\pi_{1}^{-}(x\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,\Delta^{+}_{y,A}))\\
&&\text{where }\Delta^{+}_{y,A}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\lor\neg A){}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{1}\!\!{}^{+}(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(z:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,(y\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{+}}}\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:\neg A{}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{+}z))\end{array}}

we can note that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\pitchfork^{+}_{A}:(A\lor\neg A){}^{\oplus}
holds.
However,
\pitchfork^{+}_{A}
is not intuitionistic,
due to the fact that
there is a useful occurrence of the negative counterfactual
x
.

The intuitionistic fragment is stable by reduction:

Proposition 8

(Subject reduction for
PRJ
)
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t:P
and
t\to s
.
Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}s:P
.

Proof.

If
X
is a set of variables,
we say that a term
t
is
X
-intuitionistic

if it is intuitionistic and, furthermore,
it has no
useful
free occurrences of variables in
X
.
We write
\Gamma\vdash^{X}_{\textup{PRJ}}t:P
,
if the judgment is derivable in
PRK
and
t
is
X
-intuitionistic.
The statement of subject reduction is generalized as follows:

\Gamma\vdash^{X}_{\textup{PRJ}}t:P
and
t\to s
,
then
\Gamma\vdash^{X}_{\textup{PRJ}}s:P
.

The interesting case is the
\beta
rule for positive weak proofs,

(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\ominus}}.\,t)\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\circ}}}t\{x:=s\}
.
By hypothesis,
\Gamma\vdash^{X}_{\textup{PRJ}}(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{x:A{}^{\ominus}}.\,t)\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s:A{}^{+}
.
This judgment can only be derived from the
I
{}_{\circ}^{+}
rule,
so
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash^{X}_{\textup{PRJ}}t:A{}^{+}
.
Moreover,
x
is a negative counterfactual,
so there cannot be useful free occurrences of
x
in
t
,
which means that
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash^{X\cup\{x\}}_{\textup{PRJ}}t:A{}^{+}
.
On the other hand,
s
lies inside a positive application,
so it is not necessarily
X
-intuitionistic,

i.e.
we only know
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:A{}^{\ominus}
.
The key observation is that all the copies of
s
on the
right-hand side
t\{x:=s\}
must be useless,
because all the occurrences of
x
in
t
are useless.
More precisely,
from
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash^{X\cup\{x\}}_{\textup{PRJ}}t:A{}^{+}

and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:A{}^{\ominus}

one concludes
\Gamma\vdash^{X}_{\textup{PRJ}}t\{x:=s\}:P

by induction on
t
.
∎

Reasoning principles in
PRJ
differ from those of
PRK
.
For example, if
P
is a weak formula,
a sequent
\Gamma,x:P\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:Q
valid in
PRK

can always be
contraposed
to a sequent of the form

\Gamma,y:Q{}^{\sim}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t^{\prime}:P{}^{\sim}
.
The analogous of this contraposition principle in
PRJ

depends on the sign of
P
.
If
P
is positive,
i.e.

P=A{}^{\oplus}

the sequent
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t:Q

can always be contraposed to
\Gamma,y:Q{}^{\sim}\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t^{\prime}:A{}^{\ominus}
.
But if
P
is negative,
i.e.

P=A{}^{\ominus}

the sequent
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t:Q

can only be contraposed to
\Gamma,y:Q{}^{\sim}\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t^{\prime}:A{}^{\oplus}

if there are no useful occurrences of
x
in
t
.

The following theorem is an analog of Thm.
3

for
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}
.
We omit the proof for lack of space;
see Sections
F
,

G
,
H

in the appendix.

Theorem 9

(Intuitionistic refinement)
.

A{}^{\oplus}_{1},\ldots,A{}^{\oplus}_{n}\vdash B{}^{\oplus}
holds in
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}

if and only if

A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash B
holds in intuitionistic
second-order logic.

6
Canonicity

In sequent calculus and natural deduction,
an
indirect proof
(
e.g.
with cuts),
can always be mechanically converted into
a
canonical proof
(
e.g.
cut-free), in which the justification
