for the conclusion is immediately available,
as is known from the
works of Gentzen
[
19
]

and Prawitz
[
40
]
.
Its philosophical importance is that the validity of an indirect proof
can thus be justified by understanding it as a notation for describing
a canonical proof. A practical consequence is that
an explicit witness may be extracted from a proof of existence.

In this section, we formulate a
canonicity
result
strengthening those of
[
4
]
.
We start by introducing some nomenclature.

Neutral terms
(
e,\ldots
) and

normal terms
(
f,\ldots
) are
given by

e::=x\mid e\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}f\mid f\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}e\mid\pi_{i}^{\pm}(e)\mid\delta^{\pm}e\,[_{x}.f][_{x}.f]\mid e@^{\pm}f\mid\varrho^{\pm}{e}[_{x;y}.{f}]\mid\mathsf{M}^{\pm}e\mid e@^{\pm}A\mid\nabla^{\pm}\,e[_{(x,\alpha)}.f]\mid e\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,f

and

f::=e\mid\langle f,f\rangle^{\pm}\mid\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\pm}(f)\mid\lambda^{\pm}_{x}.{f}\mid(f\,\bm{;}\!^{\pm}f)\mid\mathsf{N}^{\pm}f\mid\lambda^{\pm}_{\alpha}.\,f\mid\langle A,f\rangle^{\pm}\mid\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{x}.\,f
.
Terms built with an introduction rule:

\langle t,s\rangle^{\pm}
,

\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\pm}(t)
,

\lambda^{\pm}_{x}.{t}
,

(t\,\bm{;}\!^{\pm}s)
,

\mathsf{N}^{\pm}t
,

\lambda^{\pm}_{\alpha}.\,t
,

\langle A,t\rangle^{\pm}
,

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{x}.\,t

are called
canonical
. Then
(
see Thm.
54
in the appendix for details
):

Theorem 10

(Canonicity)
.

1.

If
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P
,
then
t
reduces to a canonical normal form
f

such that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}f:P
.

2.

If
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P
, where
P
is weak,
then a canonical normal form
f
can be effectively found
such that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{(x:P{}^{\sim})}.\,f:P

Note that this canonicity theorem applies to closed terms only,
so there is no need to include commutative conversions,
such as

\delta{}^{+}x\,[_{y}.t][_{z}.s]\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,u\to\delta{}^{+}x\,[_{y}.t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,u][_{z}.s\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,u]
,
to unblock redexes.
The preceding canonicity result extends and strengthens Thm. 35 of
[
4
]
.
The first part of the theorem confirms the intuition,
mentioned in the introduction,
that canonical proofs of strong propositions
are always constructed with an introduction rule
for the corresponding logical connective,
while canonical proofs of weak propositions
proceed by
reductio ad absurdum
.
For example, if
\vdash t:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}

is the
strong
proof of a conjunction,
its normal form must be a pair
\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle{}^{+}

and we know that
\vdash t_{i}:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}
must hold for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
On the other hand, if
\vdash s:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}

is the
weak
proof of a conjunction,
we can only assure that
its normal form is of the form
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{\ominus})}.\,s^{\prime}
,
where
x:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{\ominus}\vdash s^{\prime}:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}
.
The second part of the theorem provides the stronger guarantee
that, in such case, one can compute a
canonical

s^{\prime\prime}

such that

x:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{\ominus}\vdash s^{\prime\prime}:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}
,
so in particular one has that
s^{\prime\prime}=\langle s_{1},s_{2}\rangle{}^{+}

and that

x:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{\ominus}\vdash s_{i}:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}

for all
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Canonicity can also
be used to obtain a (weak) form of
disjunctive property
.
In particular, from
\vdash t:(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{\oplus}

one can always find an
i\in\{1,2\}
and a term

\vdash\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{+}(t^{\prime}):(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{\oplus}

such that
x:(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{\ominus}\vdash t^{\prime}:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}
.
Similarly, a (weak) form of
witness extraction
can
be obtained:
given
\vdash t:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{\oplus}

one can always find a term

\vdash\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{\ominus})}.\,\langle B,t^{\prime}\rangle{}^{+}:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{\oplus}

where
x:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{\ominus}\vdash t^{\prime}:A\{\alpha:=B\}{}^{\oplus}
.

Furthermore,
canonicity provides a purely syntactic proof of the consistency of
PRK
4
4

4

Another
way to prove consistency is using Thm.
3
,
noting that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\alpha{}^{+}
implies
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\alpha
.
.
Note, for example, that if
\alpha
is a base type,
there is no canonical term
t
such that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:\alpha{}^{+}
.

7
Conclusion

In this paper we have extended the
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
-calculus of
[
4
]

to incorporate implication and co-implication,
as well as second-order quantifiers.
From the logical point of view, this extension of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

refines classical second order logic
(Thm.
3
).
From the computational point of view, it is

confluent
(Thm.
3
)
and
strongly normalizing
(Section
4
).
These ingredients constitute a computational interpretation for
second-order classical logic.
We have identified a well-behaved subset of the system,
called
\lambda^{\textup{PRJ}}
, that

refines intuitionistic second-order logic
(Thm.
9
).
We have also formulated a
canonicity
(Thm.
10
)
result that strengthens results of previous works.
One noteworthy property of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

is that both typing and reduction rules are fully symmetric
with respect to the operation
that flips signs and exchanges the roles of dual connectives,
while still being confluent.

Related work

The “
I
{}_{\circ}^{+}
”/“
I
{}_{\circ}^{-}
” rules in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

encode a primitive variant of
consequentia mirabilis
(
(\neg A\to A)\to A
),
while the “
\lambda
” rule in
Barbanera and Berardi’s calculus
[
3
]
,
as well as the “
\mu
” rule in
Parigot’s
\lambda\mu
-calculus
[
36
]
,
encode a primitive variant of
double negation elimination
(
\neg\neg A\to A
).
To prove
A
using double negation elimination
one may assume
\neg A
and then provide a proof of
\bot
.
This proof
cannot be canonical
,
as there are no introduction rules for the empty type.
This motivates that we instead rely on
consequentia mirabilis
.

Strong normalization proofs are often based on reducibility candidates.
Yamagata proves strong normalization for second-order
formulations of classical calculi
[
47
,
48
]
,
via reducibility candidates.
Our proof is inspired by ideas known from the literature
of logical relations and biorthogonality:
for instance,
the notions of
orthogonal
r.c.’s and
closure
of a r.c. can be traced back to Krivine’s work on classical realizability
[
27
]
,
Pitts’
\top\top
-closed logical relations
[
38
]
,
and related notions (see
e.g.

[
17
]
).
The challenging aspect of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
is the mutually recursive dependency
between
A{}^{\oplus}
and
A{}^{\ominus}
,
for which
our key reference is Mendler’s work
[
30
]
.

The problem of finding a good calculus for
classical logic has not been unquestionably settled.
Current proof assistants based on type theory, such as
Coq
,
allow classical reasoning by postulating axioms with no
computational content, which breaks canonicity.
An established classical calculus
is Parigot’s
\lambda\mu
,
whose metatheory has been thoroughly developed;
see for instance
[
15
,
12
,
28
,
43
,
44
,
37
,
26
,
25
]
.
One difference between
\lambda\mu
and
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

is that
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
computational rules are based on the standard
operation of substitution, while
\lambda\mu
is based on an
ad hoc

substitution operator.
Another difference is that the embedding of the

\lambda
-calculus into
\lambda\mu
is an inclusion,
whereas the embedding into
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
is much more convoluted.

Another established classical calculus
is Curien and Herbelin’s
[
9
]

\bar{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}
, whose study is also quite
mature; see for instance
[
39
,
24
,
16
,
46
,
10
,
2
,
1
,
31
]
.
One difference between
\bar{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}
and
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

is that
\bar{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}
is not confluent unless
a reduction strategy is fixed in the presence of a specific critical
pair, whereas
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
is orthogonal.
Another difference is that
\bar{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}
is derived
from a proof term assignment for classical sequent calculus,
while
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
is defined in natural deduction style with
four forms of judgment (given by the modes
A{}^{+}
,
A{}^{-}
,

A{}^{\oplus}
,
A{}^{\ominus}
).
Munch-Maccagnoni
[
32
]
proposes a classical calculus
by
polarizing
Curien and Herbelin’s calculus,
in such a way that the reduction strategy becomes determined by the polarities.

As mentioned in the introduction,
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
is related to Nelson’s
constructible falsity
[
33
]
.
Parigot
[
35
]

studies
free deduction
, a system for classical logic
in which natural deduction and sequent calculus can both be embedded.
Rumfitt
[
42
]
proposes
bilateral
logical systems,
in which assertion and denial judgments, with dual rules, are
formulated.
Zeilberger
[
49
]
studies a polarized logical system with
proofs and refutations distinguishing between verificationist and pragmatist
connectives.
These systems, however, do not distinguish between weak and strong
propositions, and they do not have rules analogous to
\textup{{I${}_{\circ}^{\pm}$}}/\textup{{E${}_{\circ}^{\pm}$}}
.

Future work

The merely
logical
correspondence between
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
and
other classical calculi is immediate, given the fact that
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
refines
second-order classical logic (Thm.
3
).
However, it is not obvious what their relation is from
the
computational
point of view.

In order to be able to build programming languages and proof assistants
based on the principles of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
, it would be convenient to
study dependently typed extensions of the system. It is not
a priori

clear what such an extension would look like.

It is known that, in classical logic,
reductio ad absurdum
can be
postponed, in such a way that it is used at most once,
as the last rule in the derivation
[
45
,
23
]
.
It would be interesting to see if this result can be reproduced in
PRK
.

Appendix A
Second-Order Natural Deduction

Definition 11

(Second-Order Natural Deduction)
.

Formulas are given by:

\begin{array}[]{rrl}A&::=&\alpha\mid\bot\mid A\land A\mid A\lor A\mid A\rightarrow A\mid A\ltimes A\mid\neg A\mid\forall\alpha.\,A\mid\exists\alpha.\,A\end{array}

The intuitionistic second-order natural deduction system

NJ
is given by the following inference rules.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 14.08183pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=34.55527pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{ax}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,A\vdash A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.15555pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash\bot$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=23.50816pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{E$\bot$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash A\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash B$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=61.7541pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{I$\land$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 10.26038pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A\land B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\land A_{2}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=47.36626pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{E$\land_{i}$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 10.2754pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A_{i}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.2754pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash A_{i}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=47.36624pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{I$\lor_{i}$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\lor A_{2}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash A\lor B\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,A\vdash C\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,B\vdash C$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=139.9217pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{E$\lor$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 58.05338pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash C$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 2.82637pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma,A\vdash B$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=40.71947pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{I$\rightarrow$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A\rightarrow B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash A\rightarrow B\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=78.45398pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{E$\rightarrow$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 27.2172pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash B$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash\neg A\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash B$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=69.46239pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{I$\ltimes$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 13.60063pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A\ltimes B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash A\ltimes B\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,\neg A,B\vdash C$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=110.61644pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{E$\ltimes$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 43.40074pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash C$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma,A\vdash\bot$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=32.2442pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{I$\lnot$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.51389pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lnot A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash\neg A\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=68.95094pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{E$\lnot$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 23.87694pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\bot$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash A\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha\not\in\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=79.21376pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{I$\forall$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 19.5138pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\forall\alpha.\,A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.54442pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash\forall\alpha.\,B$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=61.78645pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{E$\forall$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash B\{\alpha:=A\}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash B\{\alpha:=A\}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=61.78644pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{I$\exists$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 10.54442pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\exists\alpha.\,B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash\exists\alpha.\,A\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma,A\vdash B\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha\not\in\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma,B)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=156.74345pt\hbox{\kern 2.83339pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{E$\exists$}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 66.36192pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash B$}}}}\end{array}

The classical second-order natural deduction system
NK

is obtained by extending
NJ
with the law of excluded middle:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.18045pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=52.3609pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{lem}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A\lor\neg A$}}}}\end{array}

Furthermore, the following rules are admissible in
NJ
and
NK
.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.04167pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash A$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=27.49991pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{W}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta\vdash A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma,B\vdash A\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash B$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=78.1775pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{Cut}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 26.38046pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A$}}}}\end{array}

We write
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{NJ}}A
(respectively,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A
)
if the sequent
\Gamma\vdash A
holds in
NJ
(respectively,
NK
).

Appendix B
Subject Reduction for
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

Lemma 12
.

The substitution rule is admissible in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 57.2344pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:P$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=152.13872pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{\footnotesize{{{{SubT}}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\{\alpha:=A\}\vdash t\{\alpha:=A\}:P\{\alpha:=A\}$}}}}\end{array}

Proposition 13

(Subject Reduction)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P

and
t\xrightarrow{}s
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:P
.

Proof.

This extends the proof of Prop. 24 from
[
4
]
.
The proof follows the usual methodology, by case analysis on
the derivation of the reduction step.
We focus on the more interesting cases, namely the
second-order quantifiers. We only study the positive cases,
the negative cases being symmetric.

\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\forall}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\exists}}
rule.

Let:

\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 19.49872pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\pi$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=44.69771pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t:B{}^{\oplus}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha\not\in\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma)

I
{}_{\forall}^{+}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,t:\forall\alpha.\,B{}^{+}

E
{}_{\forall}^{+}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,t)@{}^{+}A:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}

Then:

\displaystyle\penalty\pi

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t:B{}^{\oplus}

SubT

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t\{\alpha:=A\}:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}

Note that
\Gamma=\Gamma\{\alpha:=A\}
holds
since
\alpha\not\in\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma)
.

\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\exists}}\,/\,\mathsf{\beta{}^{-}_{\forall}}
rule.

Let:

\displaystyle\penalty\pi

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}

I
{}_{\exists}^{+}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\langle A,t\rangle^{\pm}:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{+}

\displaystyle\penalty\pi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Gamma,x:B{}^{\oplus}\vdash s:P

E
{}_{\exists}^{+}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\nabla{}^{+}\,\langle A,t\rangle{}^{+}[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]:P

where
\alpha\not\in\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma,P)
. Then:

\displaystyle\penalty\pi

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}

\displaystyle\penalty\pi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Gamma,x:B{}^{\oplus}\vdash s:P

SubT

\displaystyle\Gamma,x:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}\vdash s\{\alpha:=A\}:P

CCut

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash s\{\alpha:=A\}\{x:=t\}:P

\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\forall}}}
rule.

Let:

\displaystyle\penalty\pi

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t:B{}^{\oplus}

I
{}_{\forall}^{+}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,t:\forall\alpha.\,B{}^{+}

\displaystyle\penalty\pi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash s:B{}^{\ominus}\{\alpha:=A\}

I
{}_{\forall}^{-}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\langle A,s\rangle{}^{-}:\forall\alpha.\,B{}^{-}

Abs

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,t)\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}\langle A,s\rangle{}^{-}:P

where
\alpha\not\in\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma)
.
Then:

\displaystyle\penalty\pi

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t:B{}^{\oplus}

SubT

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t\{\alpha:=A\}:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}

\displaystyle\penalty\pi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash s:B{}^{\ominus}\{\alpha:=A\}

Abs

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t\{\alpha:=A\}\mathrel{\bowtie_{P}}s:P

\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\exists}}}}
rule.

Symmetric to
\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{\forall}}}}
.
∎

Appendix C
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
Refines Classical Second-Order Logic

The proof that
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
refines classical second-order logic
is an extension of analogous results for
classical propositional logic in
[
4
]
.
The proof of Thm.
3

is split into two lemmas:

Classical Conservativity
(Lem.
14
)
proves the implication
1\implies 2
, and

Classical Embedding
(Lem.
18
)
proves the implication
2\implies 1
.

Lemma 14

(Classical Conservativity)
.

If
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\vdash Q
holds in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
,
then
\iota(P_{1}),\ldots,\iota(P_{n})\vdash\iota(Q)

holds in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\vdash Q
,
it suffices to show that all rules of
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

are, from the logical point of view,
admissible in classical second-order logic.
The formal target system is
the classical second-order natural deduction system
NK

described in Section
A

of the appendix.

Recall that double negation
A\leftrightarrow\neg\neg A
,
as well as all De Morgan’s laws hold in classical second-order logic;
for example

\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\neg(A\land B)\leftrightarrow\neg A\lor\neg B

and

\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\neg\forall\alpha.\,A\leftrightarrow\exists\alpha.\,\neg A
.
Hence the proof can be reduced to studying the rules for positive
connectives, noting that the proof for the
negative rule for the dual connective must be symmetric:

1.

Ax
:
Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}x:P

where
(x:P)\in\Gamma
.
Then
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\iota(P)
by
ax
.

2.

Abs
:
Note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A\rightarrow\neg A\rightarrow\iota(P)
.

3.

I
{}_{\circ}^{+}
:
Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,t:A{}^{\oplus}

be derived from
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:A{}^{+}
.
By IH we have that
\iota(\Gamma),\neg A\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A

which,
classically
, implies

\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A
.

4.

E
{}_{\circ}^{+}
:
Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,s:A{}^{+}

be derived from
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:A{}^{\oplus}

and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:A{}^{\ominus}
.
By IH on the first premise, we have that
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A
,
as required.

5.

I
{}_{\land}^{+}
:
Note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A\rightarrow B\rightarrow(A\land B)
.

6.

E
{}^{+}_{\land{i}}
:
Note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}(A_{1}\land A_{2})\rightarrow A_{i}
.

7.

I
{}^{+}_{\lor{i}}
:
Note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A_{i}\rightarrow(A_{1}\lor A_{2})
.

8.

E
{}_{\lor}^{+}
:
Let

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\delta{}^{+}t\,[_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.s][_{y:B{}^{\oplus}}.u]:P

be derived from
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(A\lor B){}^{+}

and
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:P

and
\Gamma,y:B{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}u:P
.
By IH,
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A\lor B

and
\iota(\Gamma),A\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\iota(P)

and
\iota(\Gamma),B\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\iota(P)
,
which imply
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\iota(P)

by
E
\lor
.

9.

I
{}_{\rightarrow}^{+}
:
Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\lambda{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.{t}:(A\rightarrow B){}^{+}

be derived from
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:B{}^{\oplus}
.
By IH,
\iota(\Gamma),A\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}B
,
which implies
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A\rightarrow B

by
I
\rightarrow
.

10.

E
{}_{\rightarrow}^{+}
:
Note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow A\rightarrow B
.

11.

I
{}_{\ltimes}^{+}
:
Note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\neg A\rightarrow B\rightarrow(A\ltimes B)
.

12.

E
{}_{\ltimes}^{+}
:
Note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}(A\ltimes B)\rightarrow(\neg A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow C
.

13.

I
{}_{\lnot}^{+}
:
Note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\neg A\rightarrow\neg A
.
For the dual rule
I
{}_{\lnot}^{-}
,
note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A\rightarrow\neg\neg A
.

14.

E
{}_{\lnot}^{+}
:
Note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\neg A\rightarrow\neg A
.
For the dual rule
E
{}_{\lnot}^{-}
,
note that
\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\neg\neg A\rightarrow A
,
which holds
classically
.

15.

I
{}_{\forall}^{+}
:
Let

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,t:(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{+}

be derived from

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:A{}^{\oplus}
,
where
\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma)
.
By IH,
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}A
.
Moreover, note that
\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\iota(\Gamma))

since
\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma)
.
Hence by
I
\forall
we have that

\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\forall\alpha.\,A
.

16.

E
{}_{\forall}^{+}
:
Let

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t@{}^{+}A:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}

be derived from

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{+}
.
By IH,
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\forall\alpha.\,B
,
which implies

\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}B\{\alpha:=A\}

by
E
\forall
.

17.

I
{}_{\exists}^{+}
:
Let

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\langle A,t\rangle{}^{+}:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{+}

be derived from

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:B{}^{\oplus}\{\alpha:=A\}
.
By IH,

\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}B\{\alpha:=A\}
,
which implies

\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\exists\alpha.\,B

by
I
\exists
.

18.

E
{}_{\exists}^{+}
:
Let

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\nabla{}^{+}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]:P

be derived from

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{+}

and

\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:P
,
where

\alpha\not\in\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma,P)
.
By IH we have that

\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\exists\alpha.\,A

and

\iota(\Gamma),A\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\iota(P)
.
Moreover, note that
\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\iota(\Gamma),\iota(P))
.
Hence by
E
\exists
we have
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}\iota(P)
,
as required.

∎

Before proving embedding, we recall some auxiliary lemmas
from
[
4
]
:

Lemma 15

(Excluded middle and non-contradiction)
.

For every pure type
A
,
there exist terms
\pitchfork^{+}_{A}
and
\pitchfork^{-}_{A}

such that:

1.

Excluded middle.

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\pitchfork^{+}_{A}:(A\lor\neg A){}^{\oplus}

2.

Non-contradiction.

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\pitchfork^{-}_{A}:(A\land\neg A){}^{\ominus}

Proof.

For
excluded middle
, take:

\!\!{\small\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l}\pitchfork^{+}_{A}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(A\lor\neg A){}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{2}\!\!{}^{+}(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:\neg A{}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{+}\pi_{1}^{-}(x\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,\Delta^{+}_{y,A}))\\
\Delta^{+}_{y,A}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\lor\neg A){}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{1}\!\!{}^{+}(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(z:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,(y\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{+}}}\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:\neg A{}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{+}z))\end{array}}

For
non-contradiction
, take:

\!\!{\small\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l}\pitchfork^{-}_{A}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(x:(A\land\neg A){}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{2}\!\!{}^{-}(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(y:\neg A{}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{-}\pi_{1}^{+}(x\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\Delta^{-}_{y,A}))\\
\Delta^{-}_{y,A}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\land\neg A){}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{1}\!\!{}^{-}(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(z:A{}^{\oplus})}.\,(y\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{-}}}\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:\neg A{}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{-}z))\end{array}}

∎

Lemma 16

(Classical contraposition)
.

If
P
is weak and

\Gamma,x:P\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:Q
,
there is a term
{\mathtt{cc}_{x}^{y}(t)}

such that

\Gamma,y:Q{}^{\sim}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}{\mathtt{cc}_{x}^{y}(t)}:P{}^{\sim}
.

Proof.

As in
[
4
]
, it suffices to take:

{\mathtt{cc}_{x}^{y}(t)}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\begin{cases}\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.\,(t\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{-}}}y)&\text{if $P=A{}^{\oplus}$}\\
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(x:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,(t\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{+}}}y)&\text{if $P=A{}^{\ominus}$}\end{cases}

∎

Lemma 17

(Weak negation)
.

1.

Weak negation introduction
:
If
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:A{}^{\ominus}
,
there is a term
\mathsf{N}{}^{\oplus}t

such that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\mathsf{N}{}^{\oplus}t:(\neg A){}^{\oplus}
.

2.

Weak negation elimination
:
If
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\neg A){}^{\oplus}
,
there is a term
\mathsf{M}{}^{\oplus}t

such that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\mathsf{M}{}^{\oplus}t:A{}^{\ominus}
.

Proof.

For
weak negation introduction
,
let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:A{}^{\ominus}
and take:

\mathsf{N}{}^{\oplus}t\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\neg A){}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{+}t

For
weak negation elimination
,
let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\neg A){}^{\oplus}
and take:

\mathsf{M}{}^{\oplus}t\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{M}{}^{+}(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(\neg A){}^{\oplus}}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{-}x)\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,x

∎

Lemma 18

(Classical Embedding)
.

If
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\vdash Q
holds in
classical second-order logic,
then
P{}^{\oplus}_{1},\ldots,P{}^{\oplus}_{n}\vdash Q
holds in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction of the derivation
of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash_{\mathsf{NK}}B

in the classical second-order natural deduction system
NK
.

1.

ax
:
Let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash A_{i}
be derived from the
ax
rule.
Then
x_{1}:A_{1}{}^{\oplus},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}x_{i}:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}

by the
Ax
rule.

2.

I
\land
:
Suppose by IH that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:A{}^{\oplus}
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:B{}^{\oplus}
.
Take:

\langle t,s\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\land B){}^{\ominus}}.\,\langle t,s\rangle{}^{+}

Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\langle t,s\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}:(A\land B){}^{\oplus}
.

3.

E
\land_{i}
:
Suppose by IH that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{\oplus}

and let
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Let
\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t)
be the term:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A_{i}{}^{\ominus})}.\,\pi_{i}^{+}(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{-}(x))\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x

Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t):A_{i}{}^{\oplus}
.

4.

I
\lor_{i}
:
Suppose by IH that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}

for some
i\in\{1,2\}
. Take:

\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{+}(t)

Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t):(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{\oplus}
.

5.

E
\lor
:
Suppose by IH that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(A\lor B){}^{\oplus}

and

\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:C{}^{\oplus}

and

\Gamma,x:B{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}u:C{}^{\oplus}
.
Let
\delta^{\mathcal{C}}t\,[_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.s][_{(x:B{}^{\oplus})}.u]

be the term:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:C{}^{\ominus})}.\,(\delta{}^{+}(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\lor B){}^{\oplus})}.\,\langle{\mathtt{cc}_{x}^{y}(t)},{\mathtt{cc}_{x}^{y}(s)}\rangle{}^{-})\,[_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.s][_{(x:B{}^{\oplus})}.u]\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y)

Then

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\delta^{\mathcal{C}}t\,[_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.s][_{(x:B{}^{\oplus})}.u]:C{}^{\oplus}
.

6.

I
\rightarrow
:
Suppose by IH that
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:B{}^{\oplus}
.
Take:

\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.\,t\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\to B){}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.{t}

Note that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.\,t:(A\rightarrow B){}^{\oplus}
.

7.

E
\rightarrow
:
Suppose by IH that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(A\rightarrow B){}^{\oplus}

and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}s:A{}^{\oplus}
.
Let
t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,s
be the term:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:B{}^{\ominus})}.\,(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\to B){}^{\oplus})}.\,(s\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}x))@{}^{+}s\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x)

Note that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,s:B{}^{\oplus}
.

8.

I
\ltimes
:
Suppose by IH that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\neg A){}^{\oplus}

and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:B{}^{\oplus}
.
Take:

(t\,\bm{;}^{\mathcal{C}}s)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\ltimes B){}^{\ominus})}.\,(\mathsf{M}{}^{\oplus}t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{+}s)

Note that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}(t\,\bm{;}^{\mathcal{C}}s):(A\ltimes B){}^{\oplus}
.

9.

E
\ltimes
:
Suppose by IH that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(A\ltimes B){}^{\oplus}

and

\Gamma,x:(\neg A){}^{\oplus},y:B{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:C{}^{\oplus}
.
Let:

\varrho^{\mathcal{C}}{t}[_{x;y}.{s}]\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{z:C{}^{\ominus}}.\,(\varrho^{+}{(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{\underline{\,\,\,}}.\,\lambda{}^{-}_{x_{0}:A{}^{\ominus}}.{\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{y:B{}^{\oplus}}.\,(s^{\prime}\mathrel{\bowtie_{B{}^{-}}}z)}))}[_{x_{0}:A{}^{\ominus};y:B{}^{\oplus}}.{s^{\prime}}]\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,z)

where
s^{\prime}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,s\{x:=\mathsf{N}{}^{\oplus}x_{0}\}
.
Note that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\varrho^{\mathcal{C}}{t}[_{x;y}.{s}]:C{}^{\oplus}
.

10.

I
\lnot
:
Suppose by IH that

\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:\bot{}^{\oplus}
.
We encode falsity as
\bot\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\alpha_{0}\land\neg\alpha_{0}

for some fixed base type
\alpha_{0}
.
With this encoding of falsity,
recall that
\vdash\pitchfork^{-}_{\alpha_{0}}:\bot{}^{\ominus}

from Lem.
15
.
Take:

\Lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.\,t\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(\neg A){}^{\ominus}}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{+}({\mathtt{cc}_{x}^{y}(t)}\{y:=\pitchfork^{-}_{\alpha_{0}}\})

Note that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\Lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.\,t:(\neg A){}^{\oplus}
.

11.

E
\lnot
:
Suppose by IH that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\neg A){}^{\oplus}

and

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:A{}^{\oplus}
.
Take:

t\texttt{{\#}}^{\mathcal{C}}s\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,t\mathrel{\bowtie_{\bot{}^{\oplus}}}\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:A{}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{-}s

Note that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t\texttt{{\#}}^{\mathcal{C}}s:\bot{}^{\oplus}
.

12.

I
\forall
:
Suppose by IH that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:A{}^{\oplus}
with
\alpha\not\in\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma)
.
Take:

\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}.\,t\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,t

Note that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}.\,t:(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{\oplus}
.

13.

E
\forall
:
Suppose by IH that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus}
.
Let
t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,A
be the term:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(B\{\alpha:=A\}){}^{\ominus})}.\,(t^{\prime}@{}^{+}A\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x)

where
t^{\prime}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus})}.\,\langle A,x\rangle{}^{-}
.
Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,A:B\{\alpha:=A\}{}^{\oplus}
.

14.

I
\exists
:
Suppose by IH that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(B\{\alpha:=A\}){}^{\oplus}
.
Take:

\langle A,t\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{\ominus})}.\,\langle A,t\rangle{}^{+}

Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\langle A,t\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus}
.

15.

E
\exists
:
Suppose by IH that

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{\oplus}

and

\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}s:B{}^{\oplus}

with
\alpha\not\in\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma,P)
.
Let
\nabla^{\mathcal{C}}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]
be the term:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:B{}^{\ominus})}.\,(\nabla{}^{+}\,t^{\prime}[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y)

where
t^{\prime}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{\oplus})}.\,\lambda{}^{-}_{\alpha}.\,{\mathtt{cc}_{x}^{y}(s)}
.
Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\nabla^{\mathcal{C}}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]:B{}^{\oplus}
.

16.

E
\bot
:
Suppose by IH that
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:\bot{}^{\oplus}
.
Then

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{\oplus}}}\pitchfork^{-}_{\alpha_{0}}:A{}^{\oplus}
.

17.

lem
:
Note that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\pitchfork^{+}_{\alpha_{0}}:(A\lor\neg A){}^{\oplus}

by Lem.
15
.

∎

Appendix D
Encoding Data Types in
\lambda^{\textup{PRK}}

In this section we show, as examples,
that the Böhm–Berarducci encoding
for disjunction and existential quantification
in terms of universal quantification and implication
allows to prove the positive introduction rules
(
I
{}^{+}_{\lor{i}}
and
I
{}_{\exists}^{+}
), as well as weak variants
of the elimination rules (
E
{}_{\lor}^{+}
and
E
{}_{\exists}^{+}
).
Furthermore, these constructions simulate the corresponding
reduction rules (
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\lor}}
and
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\exists}}
).

D.1
Encoding Disjunction

Define

A_{1}\lor A_{2}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\forall\alpha.\,((A_{1}\to\alpha)\to(A_{2}\to\alpha)\to\alpha)
.
Then positive typing rules for disjunction,
analogous to
I
{}^{+}_{\lor{i}}
and the weak variant of
E
{}_{\lor}^{+}
,
are derivable, and their constructions simulate the
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\lor}}
rule.
Let:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}X_{B}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&(A_{1}\to B)\to(A_{2}\to B)\to B\\
X&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&(A_{1}\to\alpha)\to(A_{2}\to\alpha)\to\alpha\\
X^{\prime}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&(A_{2}\to\alpha)\to\alpha\\
Y_{i}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&A_{i}\to\alpha\\
\end{array}

\bullet
Typing rule
I
{}^{+}_{\lor{i}}
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}
. Take:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l}\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{+}(t)&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:X{}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(y_{1}:Y_{1}{}^{\oplus})}.{\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:X^{\prime}{}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(y_{2}:Y_{2}{}^{\oplus})}.{\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(z:\alpha{}^{\ominus})}.\,u}}\\
u&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&y_{i}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:Y_{i}{}^{\oplus})}.\,(t\,\bm{;}z))@{}^{+}t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,z\end{array}

Then
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{+}(t):(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{+}
.

\bullet
Weak variant of the typing rule
E
{}_{\lor}^{+}
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{+}

and
\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}\vdash s_{i}:B{}^{\oplus}

for each
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Take:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\delta{}^{+}t\,[_{x_{1}}.s_{1}][_{x_{2}}.s_{2}]&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:B{}^{\ominus})}.\,(t^{\prime}@{}^{+}r_{1}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,p@{}^{+}r_{2}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y)\\
t^{\prime}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&t@{}^{+}B\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:X_{B}{}^{\oplus})}.\,(r_{1}\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}p))\\
r_{i}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A_{i}\to B){}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda_{(x_{i}:A_{i}{}^{\oplus})}.\,s_{i}\\
p&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:((A_{2}\to B)\to B){}^{\oplus})}.\,(r_{2}\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}y)\end{array}

Then

\Gamma\vdash\delta{}^{+}t\,[_{x_{1}}.s_{1}][_{x_{2}}.s_{2}]:B{}^{\oplus}
.

\bullet
Computation rule
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\lor}}
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}

and
\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}\vdash s_{i}:B{}^{\oplus}

for each
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\delta{}^{+}\mathsf{in}_{i}(t)\,[_{x_{1}}.s_{1}][_{x_{2}}.s_{2}]\\
\mathrel{\xrightarrow{}\!\!{}^{*}\,\,}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:B{}^{\ominus})}.\,(r_{i}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}}.\,(t\,\bm{;}y))@{}^{+}t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y)\\
\mathrel{\xrightarrow{}\!\!{}^{*}\,\,}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:B{}^{\ominus})}.\,(s_{i}\{x_{i}:=t\}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y)\\
\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\eta_{\circ}}}&s_{i}\{x_{i}:=t\}\end{array}

D.2
Encoding Existential Quantification

Define

\exists\alpha.\,A\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\forall\beta.\,(\forall\alpha.\,(A\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow\beta)
.
(Note that in our notation quantifiers are of higher precedence than
binary connectives).
Then positive typing rules for existential quantification,
analogous to
I
{}_{\exists}^{+}
and a weak variant of
E
{}_{\exists}^{+}
,
are derivable, and their constructions simulate the
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\exists}}
rule.
Let:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}X_{B}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&(\forall\alpha.\,(A\to B))\to B\\
Y_{B}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\forall\alpha.\,(A\to B)\\
X&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&X_{\beta}\\
Y&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&Y_{\beta}\\
\end{array}

\bullet
Typing rule
I
{}_{\exists}^{+}
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:(A\{\alpha:=B\}){}^{\oplus}
. Take:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l}\langle B,t\rangle{}^{+}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\lambda{}^{+}_{\beta}.\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:X{}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(y:Y{}^{\oplus})}.{\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(z:\beta{}^{\ominus})}.\,(t^{\prime}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,z)}\\
t^{\prime}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&y\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:Y{}^{\oplus})}.\,\langle B,u\rangle{}^{-})@{}^{+}B\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,u@{}^{+}t\\
u&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\{\alpha:=B\}\rightarrow\beta){}^{\oplus})}.\,(t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}z)\end{array}

Then
\Gamma\vdash\langle B,t\rangle{}^{+}:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{+}
.

\bullet
Weak variant of the typing rule
E
{}_{\exists}^{+}
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{+}

and
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash s:C{}^{\oplus}

with
\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma,C{}^{\oplus})
.
Take:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l}\nabla{}^{+}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(z:C{}^{\ominus})}.\,(t@{}^{+}C\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,r@{}^{+}p\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,z)\\
r&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:X_{C}{}^{\oplus})}.\,(p\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}y)\\
p&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:Y_{C}{}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\to C){}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.{s}\end{array}

Then
\Gamma\vdash\nabla{}^{+}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]:C{}^{\oplus}
.

\bullet
Computation rule
\mathsf{\beta{}^{+}_{\exists}}
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:(A\{\alpha:=B\}){}^{\oplus}

and
\Gamma,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash s:C{}^{\oplus}

with
\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma,C{}^{\oplus})
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\nabla{}^{+}\,\langle B,t\rangle{}^{+}[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]\\
\mathrel{\xrightarrow{}\!\!{}^{*}\,\,}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(z:C{}^{\ominus})}.\,(p\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{\underline{\,\,\,}}.\,\langle B,u\rangle{}^{-})@{}^{+}B\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,u@{}^{+}t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,z)\\
\mathrel{\xrightarrow{}\!\!{}^{*}\,\,}&\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(z:C{}^{\ominus})}.\,(s\{\alpha:=B\}\{x:=t\}\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,z)\\
\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\eta_{\circ}}}&s\{\alpha:=B\}\{x:=t\}\end{array}

Appendix E
Strong Normalization

E.1
Properties of Reducibility Candidates

We recall the following well-known fact from order theory (see
e.g.

[
11
, Lem. 2.30]
):

Lemma 19
.

If
(A,\leq)
is such that
\bigwedge{B}

exists for every
B\subseteq A
,
then
(A,\leq)
is a complete lattice.

Proposition 20
.

The set
\mathbf{RC}
forms a complete lattice
ordered by inclusion
\subseteq
.

Proof.

By Lem.
19
,
it suffices to show that every subset
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathbf{RC}
has
a greatest lower bound.
Let
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathbf{RC}
be a family of r.c.’s.
Take
\bigwedge\mathcal{R}:=\bigcap\{\xi\ |\ \xi\in\mathcal{R}\}
,
where, if
\mathcal{R}
is empty,
this means that
\bigwedge\mathcal{R}=\mathbf{SN}
.
Note that
\bigwedge\mathcal{R}
is a r.c. and a greatest lower bound:

1.

Closed by reduction.

Let
a\in\bigwedge\mathcal{R}
and
a\to_{\mathbf{U}}b
.
By definition,
a\in\xi
for every
\xi\in\mathcal{R}
.
Since each
\xi\in\mathcal{R}
is a r.c.,
we have that
b\in\xi
for every
\xi\in\mathcal{R}
.
Hence
b\in\bigwedge\mathcal{R}
.

2.

Complete.

Let
a\in\mathbf{SN}
be such that that

\forall b\in\mathbf{CAN}.((a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b)\implies b\in\bigwedge\mathcal{R})
.
Then note that, for each
\xi\in\mathcal{R}
one has that

\forall b\in\mathbf{CAN}.((a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b)\implies b\in\xi)

holds.
Hence, since each
\xi\in\mathcal{R}
is a r.c.,

a\in\xi
for every
\xi\in\mathcal{R}
.
Hence
a\in\bigwedge\mathcal{R}
, as required.

3.

Greatest lower bound.

By standard properties of the set-theoretic intersection,

\bigwedge\mathcal{R}=\bigcap\{\xi\ |\ \xi\in\mathcal{R}\}

is the greatest lower bound of
\mathcal{R}
with respect to inclusion.

∎

Remark 21
.

The top element of
\mathbf{RC}

is given by
\bm{\top}:=\bigwedge\varnothing=\mathbf{SN}
.
The bottom element of
\mathbf{RC}

is given by the set of terms that do not reduce to a canonical
term,
that is,

\bm{\bot}:=\{a\in\mathbf{SN}\ |\ \forall b\in\mathbf{CAN}.\ \neg(a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b)\}
.
Note that
\bm{\bot}
is a r.c. and the least element:

1.

Closed by reduction.

Let
a\in\bm{\bot}
and
a\to_{\mathbf{U}}b
.
Then
b
does not reduce to a canonical term, for otherwise

a
would reduce to a canonical term.
Hence
b\in\bm{\bot}
.

2.

Complete.

Let
a\in\mathbf{SN}
be such that

(a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b)\implies b\in\bm{\bot}
holds
for each canonical term
b\in\mathbf{CAN}
.
Observe that
b\notin\bm{\bot}
,
given that a canonical term always reduces to itself.
Hence, by the contrapositive, we conclude that

\neg(a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b)
.
Hence we have that

\forall b\in\mathbf{CAN}.\,\neg(a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b)
,
that is
a\in\bm{\bot}
, as required.

3.

Least element.

We argue that
\bm{\bot}\subseteq\xi

for each
\xi\in\mathbf{RC}
.
Indeed, let
a\in\bm{\bot}
,
and let us show that
a\in\xi
.
Note that
a
does not reduce to any canonical term, so
since
\xi
is complete, we have that
a\in\xi
.

Remark 22
.

If
a\in\mathbf{CAN}
is a canonical term,

a\in\mathbb{C}{X}
implies
a\in X
.

Lemma 23

(Operations are well-defined on r.c.’s)
.

If
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
are r.c.’s and
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in{I}}
is a set of r.c.’s,
then
\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
,
\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}
,
\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2}
,

\xi_{1}\bm{\ltimes}\xi_{2}
,
\bm{\mathord{\sim}}\xi
,
\bm{\Pi}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}
,
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}
are well-defined r.c.’s.

Proof.

First, we claim that, for any set
X\subseteq\mathbf{CAN}
,
its closure
\mathbb{C}{X}
is a reducibility candidate:

1.

Closed by reduction:
Let
a\in\mathbb{C}{X}
and
a\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}
.
We claim that
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}{X}
.
Indeed, if
a^{\prime}
reduces to a canonical term,

i.e.

a^{\prime}\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b\in\mathbf{CAN}

then also
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b
,
so
b\in X
, as required.

2.

Complete:
Let
a\in\mathbf{SN}
be such that
for every
b\in\mathbf{CAN}

we have that
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b
implies
b\in\mathbb{C}{X}
.
We claim that
a\in\mathbb{C}{X}
.
Indeed, suppose that
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b\in\mathbf{CAN}
.
Then by hypothesis
b\in\mathbb{C}{X}
.
But
b
is a canonical term and it reduces to itself
in zero steps, so
b\in X
, as required.

Second, we prove, for each each operation, that the resulting set is
a r.c.,
i.e.
closed by reduction and complete.
If
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
are reducibility candidates
then it is immediate to conclude that

\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
is a reducibility candidate,
given that the product is defined as a closure.
Similarly for the sum, co-implication product,
negation, and indexed sum,

i.e.
if
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
then
\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
;
if
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
then
\xi_{1}\bm{\ltimes}\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
;
if
\xi\in\mathbf{RC}
then
\bm{\mathord{\sim}}\xi\in\mathbf{RC}
; and
if
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in{I}}\subseteq\mathbf{RC}
then
\bm{\Sigma}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}\in\mathbf{RC}
.
The remaining operations are:

1.

Arrow.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
.
Then
\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
:

1.1

Closed by reduction:
Let
a\in\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2}
and
a\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}
.
We claim that
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2}
.
Indeed, let
b\in\xi_{1}
,
and let us check that
a^{\prime}@b\in\xi_{2}
.
Note that, by definition,
a@b\in\xi_{2}
,
and moreover
a@b\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}@b
.
Since
\xi_{2}
is closed by reduction,
a^{\prime}@b\in\xi_{2}
,
as required.

1.2

Complete:
Let
a\in\mathbf{SN}
be such that
for every
b\in\mathbf{CAN}

we have that
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b
implies
b\in\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2}
.
We claim that
a\in\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2}
.
Indeed, let
c\in\xi_{1}
and
let us show that
a@c\in\xi_{2}
.
Since
\xi_{2}
is complete, it suffices to show that
if
b\in\mathbf{CAN}
is a canonical term
and
a@c\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b

then
b\in\xi_{2}
.
Observe that any reduction

a@c\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b\in\mathbf{CAN}

must be of the form

a@c\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}(\lambda_{x}.\,a^{\prime})@c^{\prime}\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}\{x:=c^{\prime}\}\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b

with
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}\lambda_{x}.\,a^{\prime}

and
c\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c^{\prime}
.
By hypothesis,
\lambda_{x}.\,a^{\prime}\in\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2}
.
Furthermore, since
\xi_{1}
is closed by reduction,
we have that
c^{\prime}\in\xi_{1}
.
Therefore
(\lambda_{x}.\,a^{\prime})@c^{\prime}\in\xi_{2}
.
Finally, since
\xi_{2}
is closed by reduction,
we conclude that
b\in\xi_{2}
, as required.

2.

Indexed product.

Let
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in{I}}\subseteq\mathbf{RC}
.
Then
\bm{\Pi}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}\in\mathbf{RC}
:

2.1

Closed by reduction:
Let
a\in\bm{\Pi}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}
and
a\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}
.
We claim that
a^{\prime}\in\bm{\Pi}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}
.
Let
i\in I
and note that
a@\Diamond\in\xi_{i}

and
a@\Diamond\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}@\Diamond
.
Since
\xi_{i}
is closed by reduction,
a^{\prime}@\Diamond\in\xi_{i}
.
Hence
a^{\prime}@\Diamond\in\xi_{i}
for arbitrary
i\in{I}
,
which means that
a^{\prime}\in\bm{\Pi}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}
.

2.2

Complete:
Let
a\in\mathbf{SN}
be such that
for every
b\in\mathbf{CAN}

we have that
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b
implies
b\in\bm{\Pi}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}
.
We claim that
a\in\bm{\Pi}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}
.
Indeed, let
i\in I
and let us show that
a@\Diamond\in\xi_{i}
.
Since
\xi_{i}
is complete, it suffices to show that
if
b\in\mathbf{CAN}
is a canonical term
and
a@\Diamond\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b

then
b\in\xi_{i}
.
Observe that any reduction
a@\Diamond\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b

must be of the form

a@\Diamond\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}(\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a^{\prime})@\Diamond\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b

with
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a^{\prime}
.
By hypothesis,
\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a^{\prime}\in\bm{\Pi}_{i\in{I}}\,{\xi_{i}}
.
Therefore
(\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a^{\prime})@\Diamond\in\xi_{i}
.
Finally, since
\xi_{i}
is closed by reduction,
we conclude that
b\in\xi_{i}
, as required.

∎

Lemma 24

(Arrow is order-reversing on the left)
.

Let
\xi_{1}
,
\xi^{\prime}_{1}
,
\xi_{2}
, and
\xi_{3}
denote
reducibility candidates.

1.

If
\xi_{1}\subseteq\xi^{\prime}_{1}
then

(\xi^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2})\subseteq(\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2})
.

2.

If
\xi_{1}\subseteq\xi^{\prime}_{1}
then

((\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2})\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{3})\subseteq((\xi^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2})\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{3})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\xi_{1}\subseteq\xi^{\prime}_{1}
,
let
a\in(\xi^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2})
,
and let us show that
a\in(\xi_{1}\bm{\rightarrow}\xi_{2})
.
By definition, consider an arbitrary
b\in\xi_{1}

and let us show that
a@b\in\xi_{2}
.
But
b\in\xi^{\prime}_{1}
, so in fact

a@b\in\xi_{2}
.
The second item is an immediate consequence of the first.
∎

Remark 25
.

The set
\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
is non-empty.
Note that
(\bm{\bot},\bm{\bot})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.
Indeed, if
a,b\in\bm{\bot}

note that
a
and
b
are both strongly normalizing.
Moreover, all the reducts of

a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b

are of the form

a^{\prime}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b^{\prime}

with
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}
and
b\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b^{\prime}
,
because
a
and
b
do not reduce to canonical
terms, so
a^{\prime}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b^{\prime}
does not have a
redex at the root.

Lemma 26

(Reducible terms are well-defined)
.

For each type
P
and each environment
\rho
,
the set
[\![P]\!]_{\rho}
is a reducibility candidate.

Proof.

By induction on the measure
\#(P)
.
Most cases are straightforard by induction hypothesis,
using the fact that
operations on reducibility candidates (
\bm{\times}
,
\bm{+}
, etc.)
are well defined (Lem.
23
).
The interesting cases are
[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}
and
[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}
.
We study the positive case; the negative case is similar.

To see that

[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}=\bm{\mu}(\xi.((\xi\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}))\in\mathbf{RC}

note that, by IH,

[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}\in\mathbf{RC}
and

[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}\in\mathbf{RC}
.
By the Knaster–Tarski theorem (Thm.
4
),
to see that the least fixed point exists,
it suffices to show that the mapping

f(\xi)=((\xi\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})

is order-preserving.
This results from Lem.
24
.
∎

Lemma 27

(Irrelevance for reducible terms)
.

Let
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
be environments
that agree on all the free type variables of
P
.
More precisely,
suppose that

\rho(\alpha{}^{+})=\rho^{\prime}(\alpha{}^{+})

and

\rho(\alpha{}^{-})=\rho^{\prime}(\alpha{}^{-})

for every
\alpha\in\mathsf{ftv}(P)
.
Then
[\![P]\!]_{\rho}=[\![P]\!]_{\rho^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on the measure
\#(P)
.
∎

Lemma 28

(Substitution for reducible terms)
.

[\![P\{\alpha:=A\}]\!]_{\rho}=[\![P]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho},[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}]}
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on the measure
\#(P)
,
resorting to the irrelevance lemma (Lem.
27
)
in the cases of second-order quantifiers.
∎

Lemma 29

(Reducible terms of weak type)
.

The following hold:

1.

[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}=[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}

2.

[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}=[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}

Proof.

We only show the first item; the second one is similar.
Let
f(\xi)=((\xi\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})

and
g(\xi)=((\xi\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})
.
Recall that, by definition,

[\![A{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}=\bm{\mu}(f)

and

[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}=\bm{\nu}(f)
.
To prove the equation of the first item
it suffices to show that

[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}

is the least fixed point of
f
:

1.

Fixed point.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}\\
&=&g([\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}\\
&=&(([\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}\\
&=&f([\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})\end{array}

The first equality is justified because
[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}=\bm{\nu}(g)
.

2.

Least of the fixed points.

Suppose that
\xi_{0}=f(\xi_{0})
is another fixed point of
f
,
and let us show that

([\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})\subseteq\xi_{0}
.
Observe that
\xi_{0}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}
is a fixed point of
g
:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&\xi_{0}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}\\
&=&f(\xi_{0})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}\\
&=&((\xi_{0}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}\\
&=&g(\xi_{0}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\end{array}

Hence
(\xi_{0}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\subseteq\bm{\nu}(g)
,
and we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&[\![A{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}\\
&=&\bm{\nu}(g)\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}\\
&\subseteq&(\xi_{0}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}&\text{by~Lem.~\ref{lemma:rcimp_order_reversing}}\\
&=&f(\xi_{0})\\
&=&\xi_{0}\end{array}

∎

Lemma 30

(Reducible terms of opposite strong types are orthogonal)
.

([\![A{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho},[\![A{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
A
:

1.

Type variable,
A=\alpha
.

Then, indeed, we have that
(\rho(\alpha{}^{+}),\rho(\alpha{}^{-}))\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}

because, by definition,
an environment
\rho
maps each pair of type variables

\alpha{}^{+},\alpha{}^{-}
to orthogonal reducibility candidates.

2.

Conjunction,
A=A_{1}\land A_{2}
.

Let
a\in[\![(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}

and
b\in[\![(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}
.
Since these sets are reducibility candidates,
we have that
a,b\in\mathbf{SN}
.
To show that
(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\in\mathbf{SN}
,
we proceed by induction on
\#(a)+\#(b)
.
It suffices to show that all the one-step
reducts of
a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b

are strongly normalizing,
i.e.
that if

(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\to_{\mathbf{U}}c

then
c\in\mathbf{SN}
.
There are three subcases for the step:

2.1

Step internal to
a
.

That is,

(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\to_{\mathbf{U}}(a^{\prime}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)

with
a\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}
.
Since reducibility candidates are closed by reduction,
we have that

a^{\prime}\in[\![(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}

and moreover
\#(a)>\#(a^{\prime})
.
By the inner IH,
(a^{\prime}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\in\mathbf{SN}
.

2.2

Step internal to
b
.

That is,

(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\to_{\mathbf{U}}(a^{\prime}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)

with
b\to_{\mathbf{U}}b^{\prime}
.
Since reducibility candidates are closed by reduction,
we have that

b^{\prime}\in[\![(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}

and moreover
\#(b)>\#(b^{\prime})
.
By the inner IH,
(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b^{\prime})\in\mathbf{SN}
.

2.3

Step at the root.

If there is a step at the root, then,
by the forms of the left-hand sides of rewriting rules
involving
\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}
,
we know that
a
and
b
must be canonical terms.
Recall that

[\![(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}=[\![A_{1}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\times}[\![A_{2}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}

is defined as the closure of

X=\{\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\ |\ a_{1}\in[\![A_{1}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho},a_{2}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\}
.
Also, recall that any canonical term in
\mathbb{C}{X}

must be an element of
X
.
In particular,
a\in X
,
so it must be of the form
a=\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle

for some
a_{1}\in[\![A_{1}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}

and some
a_{2}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}
.

Similarly, recall that

[\![(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}=[\![A_{1}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{+}[\![A_{2}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}

is defined as the closure of

Y=\{\mathsf{in}_{i}(b^{\prime})\ |\ i\in\{1,2\},b^{\prime}\in[\![A_{i}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\}
.
Also, recall that any canonical term in
\mathbb{C}{Y}

must be an element of
Y
.
In particular,
b\in Y
,
so it must be of the form
b=\mathsf{in}_{i}(b^{\prime})

for some
i\in\{1,2\}

and some
b^{\prime}\in[\![A_{i}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}
.

Then the step at the root is of the form

(\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\mathsf{in}_{i}(b^{\prime}))\to_{\mathbf{U}}((a_{i}@b^{\prime})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(b^{\prime}@a_{i}))
.
By Lem.
29
,
note that

a_{i}\in[\![A_{i}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}=([\![A_{i}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A_{i}{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})

so
a_{i}@b^{\prime}\in[\![A_{i}{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}
.
Similarly,
by Lem.
29
,
note that

b^{\prime}\in[\![A_{i}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}=([\![A_{i}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A_{i}{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})

so
b^{\prime}@a_{i}\in[\![A_{i}{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}
.
Finally, note that by the outer IH we know that

([\![A_{i}{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho},[\![A_{i}{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.
Therefore we have that

((a_{i}@b^{\prime})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(b^{\prime}@a_{i}))\in\mathbf{SN}
,
as required.

3.

Disjunction,
A=A_{1}\lor A_{2}
.

Dual to the previous case.

4.

Implication,
A=A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
.

Let
a\in[\![(A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}

and
b\in[\![(A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}
.
As in the case of conjunction,
note that
a,b\in\mathbf{SN}

and proceed by induction on
\#(a)+\#(b)

to show that for each step
(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\to_{\mathbf{U}}c

we have
c\in\mathbf{SN}
.
The interesting case is when there is a step at the root.

If there is a step at the root, then, by the forms of
the left-hand sides of rewriting rules involving
\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}
,
we know that
a
and
b
must be canonical terms.
Recall that
[\![(A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}=[\![A_{1}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\ltimes}[\![A_{2}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}

is defined as the closure of

X=\{(b_{1}\,\bm{;}b_{2})\ |\ b_{1}\in[\![A_{1}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho},b_{2}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\}
.
Also, recall that any canonical term in
\mathbb{C}{X}

must be an element of
X
.
In particular,
b\in X
,
so it must be of the form
b=(b_{1}\,\bm{;}b_{2})

for some
b_{1}\in[\![A_{1}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}
,
and some
b_{2}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}
.

Note that there is only one rewriting rule that may apply at the root
in this case, so the step is of the form

((\lambda_{x}.\,a^{\prime})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(b_{1}\,\bm{;}b_{2}))\to_{\mathbf{U}}(a\{x:=b_{1}\}@b_{2})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(b_{2}@a\{x:=b_{1}\})

where
a=\lambda_{x}.\,a^{\prime}
.
Moreover, since

a=\lambda_{x}.\,a^{\prime}\in[\![(A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}=[\![A_{1}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A_{2}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}
,
we have that

(\lambda_{x}.\,a^{\prime})@b_{1}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}
.
Furthermore, there is a reduction step

(\lambda_{x}.\,a^{\prime})@b_{1}\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}\{x:=b_{1}\}

so, since r.c.’s are closed by reduction,
we have that
a^{\prime}\{x:=b_{1}\}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}
.

By Lem.
29
,
note that

a^{\prime}\{x:=b_{1}\}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}=([\![A_{2}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A_{2}{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})
,
so

a^{\prime}\{x:=b_{1}\}@b_{2}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}
.
Similarly, by Lem.
29
,
note that

b_{2}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}=([\![A_{2}{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![A_{2}{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})
,
so

b_{2}@a^{\prime}\{x:=b_{1}\}\in[\![A_{2}{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}
.
By the outer IH we know that

([\![A_{2}{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho},[\![A_{2}{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.
Therefore

(a^{\prime}\{x:=b_{1}\}@b_{2}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b_{2}@a^{\prime}\{x:=b_{1}\})\in\mathbf{SN}
,
as required.

5.

Co-implication,
A=A_{1}\ltimes A_{2}
.

Dual to the previous case.

6.

Negation,
A=\neg B
.

Suppose that
a\in[\![(\neg B){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}

and
b\in[\![(\neg B){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}
.
As in the case of conjunction,
note that
a,b\in\mathbf{SN}

and proceed by induction on
\#(a)+\#(b)

to show that for each step

(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\to_{\mathbf{U}}c

we have
c\in\mathbf{SN}
.
The interesting case is when there is a step at the root.

If there is a step at the root, then, by the forms of the left-hand
sides of rewriting rules involving
\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}
,
we know that
a
and
b
must be canonical terms.
Recall that
[\![(\neg B){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}=\bm{\mathord{\sim}}[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}

is defined as the closure of

X=\{\mathsf{N}a^{\prime}\ |\ a^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\}
.
Also, recall that any canonical term in
\mathbb{C}{X}
must
be an element of
X
. In particular,
a\in X
,
so it must be of the form
a=\mathsf{N}a^{\prime}

for some
a^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}
.

Similarly,
b
must be of the form
b=\mathsf{N}b^{\prime}

for some
b^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}
.

Then the step at the root is of the form

(\mathsf{N}a^{\prime}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\mathsf{N}b^{\prime})\to_{\mathbf{U}}((b^{\prime}@a^{\prime})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(a^{\prime}@b^{\prime}))
.
By Lem.
29
,
note that

a^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}=([\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![B{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})

so
a^{\prime}@b^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}
.
Similarly, by Lem.
29
,

b^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho}=([\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![B{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho})

so
b^{\prime}@a^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}
.
Finally, note that by the outer IH we know that

([\![B{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho},[\![B{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.
Therefore

(b^{\prime}@a^{\prime})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(a^{\prime}@b^{\prime})\in\mathbf{SN}
,
as required.

7.

Universal quantification,
A=\forall\alpha.\,B
.

Suppose that
a\in[\![(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}

and
b\in[\![(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}
.
As in the case of conjunction, note that
a,b\in\mathbf{SN}

and proceed by induction on
\#(a)+\#(b)

to show that for each step
(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\to_{\mathbf{U}}c

we have
c\in\mathbf{SN}
.
The interesting case is when there is a step at the root.

If there is a step at the root, then, by the forms of the left-hand
sides of rewriting rules involving
\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}
,
we know that
a
and
b
must be canonical terms.
Recall that

[\![(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{-}]\!]_{\rho}=\bm{\Sigma}_{(\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}}\,{[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-}]}}

is defined as the closure of:

X=\{\langle\Diamond,b^{\prime}\rangle\ |\ \exists{(\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}}.\,b^{\prime}~\in~[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-}]}\}

Also, recall that any canonical term in
\mathbb{C}{X}

must be an element of
X
.
In particular,
b\in X
,
so there must exist
(\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}

such that
b=\langle\Diamond,b^{\prime}\rangle

for some
b^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]}
.

Note that there is only one rewriting rule that may apply at the root
in this case, so the step is of the form

((\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a^{\prime})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\langle\Diamond,b^{\prime}\rangle)\to_{\mathbf{U}}((a^{\prime}@b^{\prime})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(b^{\prime}@a^{\prime}))

where
a=\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a^{\prime}
.
Moreover, since

a=\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a^{\prime}\in[\![(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{+}]\!]_{\rho}=\bm{\Pi}_{(\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}}\,{[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi{}^{+},\xi{}^{-}]}}
,
we have in particular that

(\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a^{\prime})@\Diamond\in[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]}
.
Furthermore, there is a reduction step

(\lambda_{\Diamond}.\,a^{\prime})@\Diamond\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}
so,
since r.c.’s are closed by reduction, we have that

a^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]}
.

By Lem.
29
,
note that

a^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]}

which means that

a^{\prime}([\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![B{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]})

so
a^{\prime}@b^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]}
.
Similarly, by Lem.
29
,
note that

b^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{\ominus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]}=([\![B{}^{\oplus}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]}\bm{\rightarrow}[\![B{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]})

so
b^{\prime}@a^{\prime}\in[\![B{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]}
.
By the outer IH,

([\![B{}^{+}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]},[\![B{}^{-}]\!]_{\rho[\alpha:=\xi_{0}{}^{+},\xi_{0}{}^{-}]})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.
Therefore

((a^{\prime}@b^{\prime})\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}(b^{\prime}@a^{\prime}))\in\mathbf{SN}
,
as required.

8.

Existential quantification,
A=\exists\alpha.\,B
.

Dual to the previous case.

∎

E.2
Adequacy of the Reducibility Model

Lemma 31

(Type erasure preserves non-termination)
.

If
|t|
is strongly normalizing with respect to
\to_{\mathbf{U}}
,
then
t
is strongly normalizing with respect to
\xrightarrow{}
.

Proof.

It is straightforward
to show that if
t\xrightarrow{}s

then
|t|\to_{\mathbf{U}}|s|
.
Hence an infinite reduction sequence

t_{1}\xrightarrow{}t_{2}\xrightarrow{}\ldots

induces an infinite reduction sequence

|t_{1}|\to_{\mathbf{U}}{}|t_{2}|\to_{\mathbf{U}}{}\ldots
.
∎

Lemma 32

(Adequacy of absurdity)
.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{RC}
be such that
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.
If
a\in\xi_{1}
and
b\in\xi_{2}

then
(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\in\xi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

First, since
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\bm{\,\perp\!\!\!\perp}

we have that
(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\in\mathbf{SN}
.
Second, to see that
(a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b)\in\xi^{\prime}
,
since
\xi^{\prime}
is complete,
it suffices to show that all the canonical reducts
of
a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b
are in
\xi^{\prime}
.
Indeed, this holds vacuously, because
a term of the form
a\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b

has no canonical reducts.
Note that
its reducts are always of the form
a^{\prime}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}b^{\prime}
,
as can be checked by inspection of all the rewriting rules
defining
\to_{\mathbf{U}}
.
∎

Lemma 33

(Adequacy of pairing)
.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
. Then:

1.

If
a_{1}\in\xi_{1}
and
a_{2}\in\xi_{2}
,
then
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\in\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
.

2.

If
a_{1}\in\xi_{1}
and
a_{2}\in\xi_{2}
,
then
(a_{1}\,\bm{;}a_{2})\in\xi_{1}\bm{\ltimes}\xi_{2}
.

Proof.

We only prove the first item; the second one is similar.
First, note that
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are both strongly normalizing
since
a_{1}\in\xi_{1}
and
a_{2}\in\xi_{2}
.
From this it is immediate to conclude
that
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\in\mathbf{SN}
.
Second, to see that
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\in\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
,
by definition of the product
\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
,
it suffices to show that all canonical reducts of
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle

are of the form
\langle a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2}\rangle

with
a^{\prime}_{1}\in\xi_{1}
and
a^{\prime}_{2}\in\xi_{2}
.
Indeed, let
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c\in\mathbf{CAN}
,
and note that the reduction must be of the form

\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}\langle a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2}\rangle=c

with
a_{1}\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}_{1}
and
a_{2}\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}_{2}
.
Since
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are closed by reduction, we have that

a^{\prime}_{1}\in\xi_{1}
and
a^{\prime}_{2}\in\xi_{2}
, as required.
∎

Lemma 34

(Adequacy of projection)
.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
.
If
a\in\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
then
\pi_{i}(a)\in\xi_{i}
.

Proof.

First we claim that
\pi_{i}(a)\in\mathbf{SN}
.
Note that
a\in\mathbf{SN}
since
a\in\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
.
By induction on
\#(a)
,
we argue that if
\pi_{i}(a)\to_{\mathbf{U}}b
then
b\in\mathbf{SN}
.
We consider two cases, depending on whether the reduction step is
internal to
a
or at the root:

1.

If the reduction step is internal to
a
,
that is
\pi_{i}(a)\to_{\mathbf{U}}\pi_{i}(a^{\prime})
with
a\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}
,
then
\#(a)>\#(a^{\prime})
.
Note that
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}

because
\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
is closed by reduction.
Hence, by IH, we have that
\pi_{i}(a^{\prime})\in\mathbf{SN}
.

2.

If the reduction step is at the root,
then the step must be of the form

\pi_{i}(\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle)\to_{\mathbf{U}}a_{i}

where
a=\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
.
Since
a=\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
is canonical
and
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\in\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
,
by definition of the product
\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
,
we have that
a_{i}\in\xi_{i}
.
Hence
a_{i}\in\mathbf{SN}
.

Second, to see that
\pi_{i}(a)\in\xi_{i}
,
since
\xi_{i}
is complete, it suffices to show that all canonical
reducts of
\pi_{i}(a)
are in
\xi_{i}
.
That is, let
\pi_{i}(a)\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c\in\mathbf{CAN}

and let us show that
c\in\xi_{i}
.
Note that the reduction sequence
\pi_{i}(a)\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c

must be of the form

\pi_{i}(a)\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}\pi_{i}(\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle)\to_{\mathbf{U}}a_{i}\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c

with
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
.
By definition of the product
\xi_{1}\bm{\times}\xi_{2}
,
this means that
a_{i}\in\xi_{i}
,
and since
\xi_{i}
is closed by reduction
we conclude that
c\in\xi_{i}
, as required.
∎

Lemma 35

(Adequacy of injection)
.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbf{RC}
, and let
i\in\{1,2\}
.
If
a\in\xi_{i}
then
\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)\in\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}
.

Proof.

First note that
a\in\mathbf{SN}
since
a\in\xi_{i}
.
From this it is immediate to conclude that
\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)\in\mathbf{SN}
.
Second, to see that
\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)\in\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}
,
by definition of the sum,
it suffices to show that all canonical reducts of
\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)

are of the form
\mathsf{in}_{i}(a^{\prime})
with
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{i}
.
Indeed, let
\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c\in\mathbf{CAN}
,
and note that the reduction must be of the form

\mathsf{in}_{i}(a)\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathsf{in}_{i}(a^{\prime})=c

with
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}
.
Since
\xi_{i}
is closed by reduction, we have that
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{i}
,
as required.
∎

Lemma 36

(Adequacy of case)
.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{RC}
.
Let
a\in\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}
,
and let
b_{1},b_{2}

be terms such that
for all
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{1}
we have
b_{1}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}\in\xi^{\prime}
,
and
for all
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{2}
we have
b_{2}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}\in\xi^{\prime}
.
Then
\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\in\xi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

First we claim that

\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\in\mathbf{SN}
.
Note that
a\in\mathbf{SN}
since
a\in\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}
.
Moreover,
b_{1}\in\mathbf{SN}

because
x\in\xi_{1}
so
b_{1}=b_{1}\{x:=x\}\in\xi^{\prime}
.
Similarly,
b_{2}\in\mathbf{SN}
.
By induction on
\#(a)+\#(b_{1})+\#(b_{2})
,
we argue that if

\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\to_{\mathbf{U}}c

then
c\in\mathbf{SN}
.
We consider four cases, depending on whether the reduction step
is internal to
a
,
internal to
b_{1}
,
internal to
b_{2}
,
or at the root:

1.

If the reduction step is internal to
a
,
that is, the step is of the form

\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\to_{\mathbf{U}}\delta a^{\prime}\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]

with
a\to_{\mathbf{U}}a^{\prime}
,
then

\#(a)+\#(b_{1})+\#(b_{2})>\#(a^{\prime})+\#(b_{1})+\#(b_{2})
.
Note that
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}
still holds
since
\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}
is closed by reduction.
Hence, by IH, we have that

\delta a^{\prime}\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\in\mathbf{SN}
.

2.

If the reduction step is internal to
b_{1}
,
that is, the step is of the form

\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\to_{\mathbf{U}}\delta a\,[_{x}.b^{\prime}_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]

with
b_{1}\to_{\mathbf{U}}b^{\prime}_{1}
,
then

\#(a)+\#(b_{1})+\#(b_{2})>\#(a)+\#(b^{\prime}_{1})+\#(b_{2})
.
Note that
b^{\prime}_{1}
still has the property that
for all
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{1}
we have
b^{\prime}_{1}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}\in\xi^{\prime}
,
because

b_{1}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}\to_{\mathbf{U}}b^{\prime}_{1}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}

and
\xi^{\prime}
is closed by reduction,
and furthermore
b_{1}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}\in\xi^{\prime}
holds by hypothesis.
Hence, by IH, we have that

\delta a\,[_{x}.b^{\prime}_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\in\mathbf{SN}
.

3.

If the reduction step is internal to
b_{2}
,
the proof is similar to the previous case.

4.

If the reduction step is at the root, then the step must be of the form

\delta\mathsf{in}_{i}(a^{\prime})\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\to_{\mathbf{U}}b_{i}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}

where
a=\mathsf{in}_{i}(a^{\prime})
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Since
a=\mathsf{in}_{i}(a^{\prime})
is canonical and

\mathsf{in}_{i}(a^{\prime})\in\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}
, by definition of the sum,
we have that
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{i}
.
Thus, by hypothesis,
b_{i}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}\in\xi^{\prime}
,
which implies that
b_{i}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}\in\mathbf{SN}
.

Second, to see that

\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\in\xi^{\prime}
,
since
\xi^{\prime}
is complete,
it suffices to show that all canonical reducts of

\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]
are in
\xi^{\prime}
.
That is, let

\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c\in\mathbf{CAN}

and let us show that
c\in\xi^{\prime}
.
Note that the reduction sequence

\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c

must be of the form

\delta a\,[_{x}.b_{1}][_{x}.b_{2}]\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}\delta\mathsf{in}_{i}(a^{\prime})\,[_{x}.b^{\prime}_{1}][_{x}.b^{\prime}_{2}]\to_{\mathbf{U}}b^{\prime}_{i}\{x:=a^{\prime}\}\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}c

where
i\in\{1,2\}

and
a\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathsf{in}_{i}(a^{\prime})

and
b_{1}\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b^{\prime}_{1}

and
b_{2}\to^{*}_{\mathbf{U}}b^{\prime}_{2}
.
Since
\mathsf{in}_{i}(a^{\prime})
is a canonical reduct of
a\in\xi_{1}\bm{+}\xi_{2}
,
by definition of the sum, we have that
