\iota(\Gamma)\vdash\neg B\{\alpha:=A\}\end{array}}\\
&\,\textup{{{W}}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\iota(\Gamma),\forall\alpha.\,B\vdash\neg B\{\alpha:=A\}\end{array}}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\vdots\\
\pi\end{array}\\
&\,\textup{{{E$\forall$}}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\iota(\Gamma),\forall\alpha.\,B\vdash\bot\end{array}}\\
&\,\textup{{{I$\forall$}}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash\neg\forall\alpha.\,B\end{array}}

30.

\textup{{E${}_{\exists}^{+}$}}\mathtt{J}
:
Let

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash^{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}_{\textup{PRJ$\star$}}\nabla{}^{+}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]:P

be derived from

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash^{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}_{\textup{PRJ$\star$}}t:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{+}

and

\Gamma,\Delta,x:A{}^{\oplus}\vdash^{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}_{\textup{PRJ$\star$}}s:P
,
where

\alpha\not\in\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma,\Delta,P)
.
By IH we have that

\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NJ}}\exists\alpha.\,A

and

\iota(\Gamma),A\vdash_{\mathsf{NJ}}\iota(P)
.
Moreover, note that
\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\iota(\Gamma),\iota(P))
.
Hence by
E
\exists
we have
\iota(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{NJ}}\iota(P)
,
as required.

∎

Lemma 52

(Intuitionistic Embedding)
.

If
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash_{\mathsf{NJ}}B

there exists a term
t

such that

x_{1}:A_{1}{}^{\oplus},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t:B{}^{\oplus}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction of the derivation
of the judgment
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash_{\mathsf{NJ}}B

in intuitionistic natural deduction.
The construction of the witnesses is, in almost all cases,
the same as in the classical case (Lem.
18
).
Here we only check that these witnesses are actually intuitionistic terms.

1.

ax
:
Let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash A_{i}
be derived from the
ax
rule.
Then
x_{1}:A_{1}{}^{\oplus},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}x_{i}:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}

by the
Ax
rule, and
x_{i}
is intuitionistic
so
x_{1}:A_{1}{}^{\oplus},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}{}^{\oplus}\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}x_{i}:A_{i}{}^{\oplus}
.

2.

I
\land
:
Recall that:

\langle t,s\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\land B){}^{\ominus}}.\,\langle t,s\rangle{}^{+}

There are no free occurrences of the negative counterfactual in

t
nor in
s
,
so in particular there are
no free useful occurrences of the negative counterfactual
in
\langle t,s\rangle{}^{+}
.
Hence
\langle t,s\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}
is intuitionistic
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\langle t,s\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}:(A\land B){}^{\oplus}
.

3.

E
\land_{i}
:
Recall that
\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t)
is defined by:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:A_{i}{}^{\ominus})}.\,\pi_{i}^{+}(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{-}(x))\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x

There are only two free occurrences
of the negative counterfactual
x

in
\pi_{i}^{+}(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A_{1}\land A_{2}){}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{-}(x))\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x
,
both of them useless.
Hence
\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t)
is intuitionistic
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t):A_{i}{}^{\oplus}
.

4.

I
\lor_{i}
:
Recall that:

\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{\ominus})}.\,\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{+}(t)

There are no free occurrences of the negative counterfactual
in
t
, so in particular there are no useful free occurrences of the
negative counterfactual in
\mathsf{in}_{i}\!\!{}^{+}(t)
.
Hence
\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t)
is intuitionistic and

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\mathcal{C}}(t):(A_{1}\lor A_{2}){}^{\oplus}
.

5.

E
\lor
:
Recall that

\delta^{\mathcal{C}}t\,[_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.s][_{(x:B{}^{\oplus})}.u]

is defined as follows,
where the two contrapositions are indeed intuitionistic:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:C{}^{\ominus})}.\,\begin{array}[t]{l}\delta{}^{+}(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\lor B){}^{\oplus})}.\,\langle{\mathtt{ic}_{x}^{y}(t)},{\mathtt{ic}_{x}^{y}(s)}\rangle{}^{-})\\
\hskip 14.22636pt[_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.s\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y]\\
\hskip 14.22636pt[_{(x:B{}^{\oplus})}.u\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y]\\
\end{array}

All the occurrences of the negative counterfactual

y
in the body are useless.
Hence
\delta^{\mathcal{C}}t\,[_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.s][_{(x:B{}^{\oplus})}.u]

is intuitionistic and

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\delta^{\mathcal{C}}t\,[_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.s][_{(x:B{}^{\oplus})}.u]:C{}^{\oplus}
.

6.

I
\rightarrow
:
Recall that:

\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.{t}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\to B){}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.{t}

There are no free occurrences of the negative counterfactual
in
\lambda{}^{+}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.{t}
.
Hence
\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.{t}
is intuitionistic and

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{(x:A{}^{\oplus})}.{t}:(A\rightarrow B){}^{\oplus}
.

7.

E
\rightarrow
:
Recall that
t@^{\mathcal{C}}s
is defined by:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:B{}^{\ominus})}.\,(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\to B){}^{\oplus})}.\,(s\,\bm{;}\!{}^{-}x))@{}^{+}s\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x)

The occurrences of the negative counterfactual
x

are useless.
Hence
t@^{\mathcal{C}}s
is intuitionistic
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t@^{\mathcal{C}}s:B{}^{\oplus}
.

8.

I
\ltimes
:
Recall that:

(t\,\bm{;}^{\mathcal{C}}s)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(A\ltimes B){}^{\ominus})}.\,(\mathsf{M}{}^{\oplus}t\,\bm{;}\!{}^{+}s)

Note that
(t\,\bm{;}^{\mathcal{C}}s)
is intuitionistic and

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}(t\,\bm{;}^{\mathcal{C}}s):(A\ltimes B){}^{\oplus}
.

9.

E
\ltimes
:
Recall that
\varrho^{\mathcal{C}}{t}[_{x;y}.{s}]
is defined by:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{z:C{}^{\ominus}}.\,(\varrho^{+}{(t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,(\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{\underline{\,\,\,}}.\,\lambda{}^{-}_{x_{0}:A{}^{\ominus}}.{\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{y:B{}^{\oplus}}.\,(s^{\prime}\mathrel{\bowtie_{B{}^{-}}}z)}))}[_{x_{0}:A{}^{\ominus};y:B{}^{\oplus}}.{s^{\prime}}]\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,z)

where
s^{\prime}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,s\{x:=\mathsf{N}{}^{\oplus}x_{0}\}
.
The occurrences of the negative counterfactual
z
are useless,
and there are no other negative counterfactuals.
Hence
\varrho^{\mathcal{C}}{t}[_{x;y}.{s}]
is intuitionistic
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\varrho^{\mathcal{C}}{t}[_{x;y}.{s}]:(\neg A){}^{\oplus}
.

10.

I
\lnot
:
Recall that:

\Lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.\,t\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{\underline{\,\,\,}:(\neg A){}^{\ominus}}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{+}({\mathtt{ic}_{x}^{y}(t)}\{y:=\pitchfork^{-}_{\alpha_{0}}\})

where the contraposition is indeed intuitionistic.
There are no occurrences of the
negative counterfactual.
Hence
\Lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.\,t
is intuitionistic
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\Lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{x:A{}^{\oplus}}.\,t:(\neg A){}^{\oplus}
.

11.

E
\lnot
:
Recall that:

t\texttt{{\#}}^{\mathcal{C}}s\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,t\mathrel{\bowtie_{\bot{}^{\oplus}}}\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:A{}^{\oplus})}.\,\mathsf{N}{}^{-}s

Note that
t\texttt{{\#}}^{\mathcal{C}}s

is intuitionistic, so
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t\texttt{{\#}}^{\mathcal{C}}s:\bot{}^{\oplus}
.

12.

I
\forall
:
Recall that:

\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}.\,t\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{\ominus})}.\,\lambda{}^{+}_{\alpha}.\,t

Note that
\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}.\,t
is intuitionistic,
so

\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\lambda^{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}.\,t:(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{\oplus}
.

13.

E
\forall
:
Recall that
t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,A
is defined by:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(x:(B\{\alpha:=A\}){}^{\ominus})}.\,(t^{\prime}@{}^{+}A\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,x)

where

t^{\prime}=t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus})}.\,\langle A,x\rangle{}^{-}
.
All the occurrences of the negative counterfactual
x

are useless. Hence
t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,A
is intuitionistic
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t\,\texttt{{@}}^{\mathcal{C}}\,A:B\{\alpha:=A\}{}^{\oplus}
.

14.

I
\exists
:
Recall that:

\langle A,t\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{\ominus})}.\,\langle A,t\rangle{}^{+}

Note that
\langle A,t\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}
is intuitionistic,
so
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\langle A,t\rangle^{\mathcal{C}}:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{\oplus}
.

15.

E
\exists
:
Recall that
\nabla^{\mathcal{C}}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]
is defined by:

\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:B{}^{\ominus})}.\,(\nabla{}^{+}\,t^{\prime}[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,y)

where
t^{\prime}=t\bullet\!\!{}^{+}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{-}_{(\underline{\,\,\,}:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{\oplus})}.\,\lambda{}^{-}_{\alpha}.\,{\mathtt{ic}_{x}^{y}(s)}
,
and where the contraposition is indeed intuitionistic.
All the occurrences of the negative counterfactual

y
are useless.
Hence
\nabla^{\mathcal{C}}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]
is intuitionistic
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}\nabla^{\mathcal{C}}\,t[_{(\alpha,x)}.s]:B{}^{\oplus}
.

16.

E
\bot
:
Note that
t\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{\oplus}}}\pitchfork^{-}_{\alpha_{0}}
is intuitionistic,
so
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRJ}}t\mathrel{\bowtie_{A{}^{\oplus}}}\pitchfork^{-}_{\alpha_{0}}:A{}^{\oplus}
.

∎

Appendix I
Canonicity

Recall that
neutral terms
(
e,\ldots
) and

normal terms
(
f,\ldots
) are:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}r@{\,}l}e&::=&x\mid e\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}f\mid f\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD_{P}}}e\mid\pi_{i}^{\pm}(e)\mid\delta^{\pm}e\,[_{x}.f][_{x}.f]\\
&\mid&e@^{\pm}f\mid\varrho{e}[_{x;y}.{f}]\mid\mathsf{M}^{\pm}e\mid e@^{\pm}A\mid\nabla^{\pm}\,e[_{(x,\alpha)}.f]\mid e\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,f\\
f&::=&e\mid\langle f,f\rangle^{\pm}\mid\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\pm}(f)\mid\lambda^{\pm}_{x}.{f}\mid(f\,\bm{;}\!^{\pm}f)\mid\mathsf{N}^{\pm}f\mid\lambda^{\pm}_{\alpha}.\,f\mid\langle A,f\rangle^{\pm}\mid\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{x}.\,f\end{array}

A typing context
\Gamma=x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n}

is
weak

if
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
are all weak types.

Canonical
terms are those terms built with an introduction rule:

\langle t,s\rangle^{\pm}\hskip 8.5359pt\mathsf{in}_{i}^{\pm}(t)\hskip 8.5359pt\lambda^{\pm}_{x}.{t}\hskip 8.5359pt(t\,\bm{;}\!^{\pm}s)\hskip 8.5359pt\mathsf{N}^{\pm}t\hskip 8.5359pt\lambda^{\pm}_{\alpha}.\,t\hskip 8.5359pt\langle A,t\rangle^{\pm}\hskip 8.5359pt\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{x}.\,t

A
capsule
is
either a variable or a term of the form
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{x}.\,f
.
A
critical context
is a context
\mathtt{H}
given by
the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}r@{\,}l}\mathtt{H}&::=&\Box\mid\mathtt{H}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}f\mid f\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}\mathtt{H}\mid\pi_{i}^{\pm}(\mathtt{H})\mid\delta^{\pm}\mathtt{H}\,[_{x}.f_{1}][_{y}.f_{2}]\mid\mathtt{H}@^{\pm}f\\
&\mid&\varrho^{\pm}{\mathtt{H}}[_{x;y}.{f}]\mid\mathsf{M}^{\pm}\mathtt{H}\mid\mathtt{H}@^{\pm}A\mid\nabla^{\pm}\,\mathtt{H}[_{(x,\alpha)}.f]\mid\mathtt{H}\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,f\mid x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,\mathtt{H}\end{array}

A typing context
\Gamma=x_{1}:P_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:P_{n}

is
weak

if
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
are all weak types.

Lemma 53

(Shape of neutral terms)
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P

where
\Gamma
is weak
and
t
is a neutral term.
Then:

1.

If
P
is strong,
then
t
is of the form
\mathtt{H}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle

where
p
is a capsule.

2.

If
P
is weak,
then
t
is either a variable
or of the form
\mathtt{H}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle
,
where
p
is a capsule.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the derivation that
t
is a neutral term:

1.

t=x
:
If
P
is weak,
i.e.

P=A{}^{\oplus}
or
P=A{}^{\ominus}
,
then we are done, given that
t
is a variable.
If
P
is strong,
i.e.

P=A^{\pm}
,
then note that this case is impossible,
since
\Gamma\vdash x:A^{\pm}

must be derived from the
Ax
rule,
so
(x:A^{\pm})\in\Gamma
, contradicting the hypothesis that

\Gamma
is weak.

2.

t=e\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}f
:
Note that
\Gamma\vdash(e\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}f):P

must be derived from the
Abs
rule,
so in particular we must have that
\Gamma\vdash e:A{}^{+}
.
By IH,
e
must be
of the form
e=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}\langle x\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,p\rangle

where
p
is a capsule.
Then
t=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}\langle x\bullet\!\!{}^{-}\,p\rangle\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}f
,
so taking
\mathtt{H}:=(\mathtt{H}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}f)
we conclude.

3.

t=f\mathrel{{\RHD\!\!\!\LHD}}e
:
Similar to the previous case, applying the IH on the judgment

\Gamma\vdash e:A{}^{-}
.

4.

t=\pi_{i}^{\pm}(e)
:
Note that
\Gamma\vdash\pi_{i}^{\pm}(e):P

must be derived from either of the rules
E
{}^{+}_{\land{i}}
or
E
{}^{-}_{\lor{i}}
,
from a judgment of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(A\land B){}^{+}

or of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(A\lor B){}^{-}
.
In any case, the type of
e
is strong so we may apply the IH to conclude that
e=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle
,
where
p
is a capsule.
Then
t=\pi_{i}^{\pm}(\mathtt{H}^{\prime}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle)

(where the signs of the projection and the weak elimination do
not necessarily match),
so taking
\mathtt{H}:=\pi_{i}^{\pm}(\mathtt{H}^{\prime})
we conclude.

5.

t=\delta^{\pm}e\,[_{x}.f_{1}][_{y}.f_{2}]
:
Similar to the previous case, noting that

\Gamma\vdash\delta^{\pm}e\,[_{x}.f_{1}][_{y}.f_{2}]:P

must be derived from either of the rules
E
{}_{\lor}^{+}
or
E
{}_{\land}^{-}
,
from a judgment of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(A\lor B){}^{+}

or of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(A\land B){}^{-}
.

6.

t=e@^{\pm}f
:
Similar to the previous case, noting that

\Gamma\vdash e@^{\pm}f:P

must be derived from either of the rules
E
{}_{\rightarrow}^{+}
or
E
{}_{\ltimes}^{-}
,
from a judgment of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(A\rightarrow B){}^{+}

or of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(A\ltimes B){}^{-}
.

7.

t=\varrho^{\pm}{e}[_{x;y}.{f}]
:
Similar to the previous case, noting that
must be derived from either of the rules
E
{}_{\ltimes}^{+}
or
E
{}_{\rightarrow}^{-}
,
from a judgment of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(A\ltimes B){}^{+}

or of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(A\rightarrow B){}^{-}
.

8.

t=\mathsf{M}^{\pm}e
:
Similar to the previous case, noting that

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{M}^{\pm}e:P

must be derived from either of the rules
E
{}_{\lnot}^{+}
or
E
{}_{\lnot}^{-}
,
from a judgment of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(\neg A){}^{+}

or of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(\neg A){}^{-}
.

9.

t=e@^{\pm}A
:
Similar to the previous case, noting that

\Gamma\vdash e@^{\pm}A:P

must be derived from either of the rules
E
{}_{\forall}^{+}
or
E
{}_{\exists}^{-}
,
from a judgment of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(\forall\alpha.\,B){}^{+}

with
P=B{}^{\oplus}\{x:=A\}
,
or from a judgment of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(\exists\alpha.\,B){}^{+}

with
P=B{}^{\ominus}\{x:=A\}
.

10.

t=\nabla^{\pm}\,e[_{(x,\alpha)}.f]
:
Similar to the previous case, noting that

\Gamma\vdash\nabla^{\pm}\,e[_{(x,\alpha)}.f]:P

must be derived from either of the rules
E
{}_{\exists}^{+}
or
E
{}_{\forall}^{-}
,
from a judgment of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(\exists\alpha.\,A){}^{+}

or of the form

\Gamma\vdash e:(\forall\alpha.\,A){}^{-}
.

11.

t=e\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,f
:
Note that
\Gamma\vdash e\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,f:P

must be derived from either of the rules
E
{}_{\circ}^{+}
or
E
{}_{\circ}^{-}
,
from a judgment of the form
\Gamma\vdash e:A{}^{\oplus}

or of the form
\Gamma\vdash e:A{}^{\ominus}
.
Since the type of
e
is weak,
by IH we have that
e
is either a variable or of the form

e=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle
,
where
p
is a capsule.
We consider these two subcases:

11.1

If
e
is a variable,
e=x
,
note first that, since
\Gamma\vdash e\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,f:P

is derived from either of the rules
E
{}_{\circ}^{+}
or
E
{}_{\circ}^{-}
,
the type of
f
must be weak.
We consider two subcases, depending on whether

f
is of the form
f=\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\mp}_{x}.\,f^{\prime}
or not:

11.1.1

If
f=\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\mp}_{x}.\,f^{\prime}
,
then
t=x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\mp}_{x}.\,f^{\prime}
,
so taking
\mathtt{H}:=\Box
we are done.

11.1.2

If
f
is not of the form
\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\mp}_{x}.\,f^{\prime}

then since
f
is a normal term and its type is weak
we know that
f
must be neutral.
Hence by IH we know that
f
must be either a variable
or of the form
f=\mathtt{H}^{\prime\prime}\langle y\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p^{\prime}\rangle
,
where
p^{\prime\prime}
is a capsule. We consider these two subcases:

11.1.2.1

If
f
is a variable,
f=y
,
then
t=x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,y

so taking
\mathtt{H}:=\Box
we are done.

11.1.2.2

If
f=\mathtt{H}^{\prime\prime}\langle y\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p^{\prime}\rangle
,
where
p^{\prime}
is a capsule,
then
t=x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,\mathtt{H}^{\prime\prime}\langle y\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p^{\prime}\rangle

(where the signs of the two weak eliminations do not
necessarily match),
so taking
\mathtt{H}:=x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,\mathtt{H}^{\prime\prime}
we are done.

11.2

If
e=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle

where
p
is a capsule,
then
t=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,f

(where the signs of the two weak eliminations do not necessarily
match),
so taking
\mathtt{H}:=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,f

we conclude.

∎

Theorem 54

(Canonicity)
.

1.

If
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P
,
then
t
reduces to a canonical normal form
f

such that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}f:P
.

2.

If
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P
, where
P
is weak,
then a canonical term
t^{\prime}
can be effectively found
such that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}^{\pm}_{(x:P{}^{\sim})}.\,t^{\prime}:P

Proof.

We prove each case:

1.

Suppose that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t:P
holds.
By strong normalization
consider the normal form
f
of
t

and by subject reduction (Thm.
3
)
note that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}f:P
.
By the characterization
of normal forms
we know that
f
is either canonical or a neutral term.
If
f
is canonical, we are done.

It suffices to argue that
f
cannot be a neutral term.
Indeed, note that
f
cannot be a variable,
since the typing context is empty.
Hence, by Lem.
53
,

f
must be of the form
\mathtt{H}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle
,
where
\mathtt{H}
is a critical context and
p
is a capsule.
But note that critical contexts do not bind variables,
so
\mathtt{H}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle
has a free variable
x
.
Hence
f=\mathtt{H}\langle x\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle
cannot be typable
under the empty typing context.
This contradicts the fact that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}f:P
.

2.

Suppose that
\vdash t:P
, where
P
is weak.
We consider the case in which
P=A{}^{\oplus}
;
if
P=A{}^{\ominus}
the proof is similar changing the signs.

By the first item of this lemma, note that
t
reduces to a
canonical normal form
f
such that
\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}f:A{}^{\oplus}
.
Since
f
is canonical,

f=\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(y:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,f^{\prime}

where
f^{\prime}
is a normal form and

y:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}f^{\prime}:A{}^{+}
.
To prove the statement of the lemma, we must show
we can find a canonical term
t^{\prime}
such that

y:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t^{\prime}:A{}^{+}
.

We claim, more in general, that if

f^{\prime}
is a normal form such that

y_{1}:A{}^{\ominus},\ldots,y_{n}:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}f^{\prime}:A{}^{+}
,
then
we can find a canonical term
t^{\prime}
such that

y:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t^{\prime}:A{}^{+}
.
We proceed by induction on the size of
f^{\prime}
.
Suppose that

y_{1}:A{}^{\ominus},\ldots,y_{n}:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}f^{\prime}:A{}^{\oplus}
.
By the characterization
of normal forms ,

f^{\prime}
is either canonical or a neutral term.
We consider these two cases:

2.1

If
f^{\prime}
is canonical:
take

t^{\prime}=f\{y_{1}:=y\}\ldots\{y_{n}:=y\}
,
which is again canonical,
and note that

y:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t^{\prime}:A{}^{+}
,
as required.

2.2

If
f^{\prime}
is a neutral term:
since
f^{\prime}
is of strong type,
by Lem.
53

we have that
f^{\prime}=\mathtt{H}\langle y^{\prime}\bullet\!\!^{\pm}\,p\rangle

where
p
is a capsule.
Since critical contexts do not bind variables,
we know that
y^{\prime}=y_{i}
for some
i\in 1..n

and since
y_{i}
is of type
A{}^{\ominus}

we have

y_{1}:A{}^{\ominus},\ldots,y_{n}:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}p:A{}^{\oplus}
.
Moreover,
p
is a capsule,

i.e.
either a variable or a weak introduction.
Note that
p
cannot be a variable, for all the variables
in
y_{1}:A{}^{\ominus},\ldots,y_{n}:A{}^{\ominus}

are of type
A{}^{\ominus}
, whereas
p
is of type
A{}^{\oplus}
.
Hence
p
must be a weak introduction,
so we know that it must be of the form

p=\mathsf{\textup{\Circle}}{}^{+}_{(z:A{}^{\ominus})}.\,f^{\prime\prime}
.
Note that

y_{1}:A{}^{\ominus},\ldots,y_{n}:A{}^{\ominus},z:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}f^{\prime\prime}:A{}^{+}
,
where
f^{\prime\prime}
is a strict subterm of
f^{\prime}
.
Hence, by IH, there exists a canonical term
t^{\prime}

such that

y:A{}^{\ominus}\vdash_{\textup{PRK}}t^{\prime}:A{}^{+}
.
This concludes the proof.

∎
